<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
For any fixed reduced word $\imath_\bullet$, every irreducible component of $\overline{S_\mu\cap T_0}$ arises as $\overline{A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)}$ for some integer tuple $n_\bullet$. Furthermore, 
for any two reduced words $\imath_\bullet$ and $ \imath'_\bullet$, if the Lusztig data $(\imath_\bullet, n_\bullet)$ and $(\imath'_\bullet, n'_\bullet)$ are related by Lusztig transform, then $\overline{A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)}= \overline{ A^{\imath'_\bullet}(n'_\bullet)}$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
If $G$ is either simply-connected or of adjoint type, then $G^\sigma$ is connected.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
If $G$ is simply-connected or adjoint and $\tau$ is a diagram automorphism of $G$, then it is well known that $G^\tau$ is connected.

If $\kappa=0$, then $\sigma=\tau$ is a diagram automorphism. The Lemma follows.
   

If $\kappa\not=0$ and $X_N^{(r)}\not= A_{2\ell}^{(2)}$, then there is an isomorphism $G^\tau\simeq G^\sigma$, given by $g\mapsto {\rm Ad}_{\epsilon^\mu}(g)$, where 
 \[ \mu=  \begin{cases}
 \check{\omega}_1 \quad   \text{if } X_N^{(r)}=         A_{2\ell-1}^{(2)}  \\
   \check{\omega}_\ell \quad   \text{if } X_N^{(r)}= D_{\ell+1}^{(2)}  
    \end{cases}  .\]
For the final case $X_N^{(r)}=A_{2\ell}^{(2)}$, we resort to the following fact: when $G$ is simply-connected or $G$ is adjoint of type $A_{2n}, D_4, E_6$, $G^\sigma$ is always connected (cf.\,\cite[\S 8.1, \S 9.8]{St}).
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rmk:surj}
 There is a direct description of $X_*(H)_\tau^+$ via the quasi-split group associated to the twiste loop group $G(\mathcal{K}_z)^\tau$, see \cite[Section 2.4]{ALRS}. Moreover, the map $X_*(H)^+\to X_*(H)^+_\tau$ is surjective when $G$ is adjoint, but NOT in general. See \cite[Lemma 2.6, Remark 2.7]{ALRS}.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop_semi_closure}
$T^w_\nu\subset \overline{T^w_\mu}$ if and only if $w^{-1}\nu\succeq w^{-1} \mu$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{key_lem}
Let $Z$ be a $\mathbb{G}_m\times H^\sigma$-orbit on $\mathscr{G}\!r$. Then $Z\subset \mathscr{G}\!r_\lambda$ for some $e_\lambda\in \overline{Z}$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
The proof of \cite[Lemma 6]{An} carries over to $\mathscr{G}\!r$. The only difference is that, for $\eta\in X_*(H)^+_\tau$, $\mathscr{G}\!r_\eta$ is a vector bundle over the core $G^\sigma\cdot e_\eta$ with respect to the $\mathbb{G}_m$-action.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{T_S_prop}
The intersection $S_\lambda\cap T_\mu$ is nonempty if and only if $\mu\preceq \lambda$. In this case, it is of pure dimension $\langle \lambda-\mu, \rho \rangle$.  Moreover, when $\mu\preceq \lambda$ and $\lambda,\mu$ are sufficiently dominant, then $\overline{S_\lambda\cap T_\mu}= \overline{\mathscr{G}\!r_\lambda\cap T_\mu }$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
For any $\eta\in X_*(H)_\tau$,  we have the following translation invariance: 
\[ S_{\lambda}\simeq S_{\lambda+\eta}, \, T_{\mu}\simeq T_{\mu+\eta},  \, \text{ and } \, S_\lambda \cap T_\mu\simeq S_{\lambda+\eta} \cap T_{\mu+\eta} . \]
We may assume $\lambda, \mu$ are sufficiently dominant so that for any $\nu\in X_*(H)_\tau$, $\mu\preceq \nu \preceq \lambda$ implies that $\nu$ is dominant.  Let $X=\overline{S_\lambda}\cap \overline{T_\mu}$.  Then, $X$ is a $\mathbb{G}_m\times H^\sigma$-stable closed subset in $\mathscr{G}\!r$. By the closure relation of semi-infinite orbits (cf.\,Proposition \ref{prop_semi_closure}), $X^{H^\sigma}$ is contained in $\{ e_\nu \mid  \mu\preceq \nu\preceq \lambda \}$. In particular, if $S_\lambda\cap T_\mu$ is nonempty, then $\mu\preceq \lambda$. 
By Lemma \ref{key_lem},  
$$X\subseteq \bigcup_{e_\nu\in X^{H^\sigma} }\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\nu \subseteq \bigcup_{\mu\preceq \nu \preceq \lambda }\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\nu.$$
Then, by the assumption on $\lambda$ and $\mu$, any $\nu$  above is dominant. Thus, $X\subseteq \overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda$, and then  $\overline{S_\lambda}\cap T_\mu\subseteq \overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap T_\mu$.

Since $\dim (\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap S_\lambda)= \dim \overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda$, $\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap S_\lambda$ is dense in $\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda$. Thus, $\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\subset \overline{S}_\lambda$. It follows that, $\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap T_\mu \subseteq \overline{S}_\lambda\cap T_\mu$. 
Therefore, if $\mu\preceq \lambda$ then
 $ \overline{S}_\lambda\cap T_\mu=\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap T_\mu$ is nonempty and is of pure dimension $\langle \lambda-\mu, \rho \rangle$. By dimension consideration, $ S_\lambda\cap T_\mu$ is open and dense in  $\overline{S}_\lambda\cap T_\mu$. It further follows that, $\overline{S_\lambda\cap T_\mu }=\overline{ \overline{S}_\lambda\cap T_\mu}$. On the other hand, ${\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap T_\mu$ is also open and dense in $\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap T_\mu$. Thus, 
\[ \overline{\mathscr{G}\!r_\lambda\cap T_\mu}= \overline{\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap T_\mu}=\overline{\overline{S}_\lambda\cap T_\mu}= \overline{S_\lambda\cap T_\mu}.\]
This concludes the proposition.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def_MV_weight}
Irreducible components of $S_\lambda\cap T_\mu$ (or $\overline{S_\lambda\cap T_\mu}$) are called \emph{MV cycles} of coweight $(\lambda, \mu)$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor_MV_Gr}
Let $\lambda\in X_*(H)_\tau^+$ and $\mu\preceq \lambda$. The components of
$\overline{ S_\lambda \cap T_\mu }$ contained in $\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_{ \lambda}$ are exactly the components of  
$\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r_\lambda\cap T_\mu}$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
Assume that $\rm{char} ({\mathrm{k}})\not= \ell$. 
The $\ell$-adic intersection cohomology $IH^*(\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda, \overline{\mathbb{Q}}_\ell)$ with coefficients in $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_\ell$ admits an algebraic action of the reductive $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_\ell$-algebraic group $\check{G}^\tau$, which is isomorphic to the highest weight module $V(\lambda)$ of $\check{G}^\tau$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm_ramified_Satake}
There exists a decomposition 
\[ IH^*(\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_\ell)\simeq \bigoplus_{\mu\in X_*(H)_\tau} H_c^{2\langle \lambda-\mu, \rho \rangle} (\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap T_\mu, \overline{\mathbb{Q}}_\ell) \]
corresponding to the weight decomposition $V(\lambda)=\bigoplus_{\mu\in X_*(H)_\nu} V(\lambda)_\mu$. Thus, the weight space $V(\lambda)_\mu$ admits a basis from the components of $\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda\cap T_\mu$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
An irreducible component $C$ of $\overline{ S_\lambda\cap T_\mu }$ is contained in $\overline{\mathscr{G}\!r}_\lambda$ if and only if $P(C)$ is contained in the Weyl polytope $P_\lambda$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Using Lemma \ref{key_lem}, the proof of this proposition is identical to the proof of \cite[Proposition 7]{An}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:semiinfinite-int}
For any $w,y\in W^\tau$, if $T_\mu^w  \cap T_\nu^y \not= \emptyset$, then $\nu\succeq_w \mu$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:semi-infinite}
For any $k\in G(\mathcal{O})^\sigma$ and $g\in G(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$, 
$D_{w \Lambda_\imath}(gk)=D_{w\Lambda_\imath}(g)$. Thus, $D_{w\Lambda_\imath}$ descends to a function on $\mathscr{G}\!r$.  
 Moreover,
 \[
 T^w_\mu=\{L\in \mathscr{G}\!r\mid D_{w \Lambda_\imath}(L)=\langle w \Lambda_\imath, a(\mu)\rangle \text{ for all $\iota\in I_\tau$}\}.
 \]
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
By the Iwasawa decomposition, 
                                                                                                                                                            
we can write $g= \dot{w} u \dot{w}^{-1}n^{\tilde{\mu}} k$, where $u\in N^{-}(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$,  $k\in G(\mathcal{O})^\sigma$, $\dot{w}$ is a $\sigma$-invariant representative of $w$ in $G$, and $\tilde{\mu}$ is a lifting of $\mu\in X_*(H)_\tau$ in $X_*(H)$. Observe that for any $v\in \overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)$,  $k\cdot v =k_0\cdot v+ tV(\Lambda_i)_{\mathcal{O}}$, where $k_0\in G^\sigma\subset G(\mathcal{O})^\sigma$ is the image of $k$ via the evaluation map $G(\mathcal{O})^\sigma\to G^\sigma$. Then, 
\[ \langle   \xi_{w \Lambda_i}  , g\cdot v  \rangle= \langle  \xi_{w \Lambda_i}, g\cdot v  \rangle  = (\epsilon^{-1}t)^{ \langle w \Lambda_i, a(\mu)  \rangle } \langle  \xi_{w \Lambda_i}, k \cdot v  \rangle   \]
lies in $   (\epsilon^{-1}t)^{\langle w\Lambda_i, a(\mu)  \rangle }  (  \langle  \xi_{w\Lambda_i}, k_0 \cdot v  \rangle + t \mathcal{O})$.
Since $ \xi_{w \Lambda_i}$ is nontrivial on $\overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)$,
 it follows that
 \[ \min_{v \in \overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)} \mathrm{val} ( \langle  \xi_{w \Lambda_\imath}  , g\cdot v  \rangle)= \langle w \Lambda_i, a(\mu) \rangle= \langle w \Lambda_\imath, a(\mu) \rangle .\]
 From this calculation, it is easy to see that $D_{w \Lambda_\imath}$ is invariant with respect to the right multiplication of $G(\mathcal{O})^\sigma$, and thus the lemma follows.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
If $A(\mu_\bullet)$ is not empty, then $P( \overline{A(\mu_\bullet) })= {\rm Conv}(\mu_\bullet)$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def_exceptional} An orbit $\imath\in I_\tau$ is said to be \emph{exceptional} if $X_N^{(r)}=A_{2l}^{(2)}$ and $\imath=\{l,l+1\}$; otherwise we say the orbit $\imath$ is \emph{standard}. Note that $a_{ij}=0$ for any distinct $i,j$ in a standard orbit $\imath$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop: A(n)} $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ consists of those $L\in \mathscr{G}\!r$ satisfying
\[
D_{\Lambda_\imath}(L)=0 \quad \text{ for all $\imath\in I_\tau$, and}
\]
\[
D_{w_{ k}^{\imath_\bullet}\Lambda_{\imath_k}}(L)=-n_km_{\imath_k}-D_{w_{k-1}^{\imath_\bullet}\Lambda_{\imath_k}}(L)-\sum_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}a_{\jmath \imath_k}D_{w_{k-1}^{\imath_\bullet}\Lambda_{\jmath}}(L) \quad \text{ for all $1\leq k\leq N$},
\]
where $m_{\imath_k}=\frac{m}{|\imath_k|}$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The condition $D_{\Lambda_\imath}(L)=0$ is determined by the fact that $L\in T_0$. We now inductively prove the remaining equalities by an induction on $k$. Note that for each $k$, by Lemma \ref{lem:semi-infinite}, $L\in T^{w^{\imath_\bullet}_k}_{\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_k}$ if and only if $D_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_k\Lambda_\jmath}(L)=\left\langle w^{\imath^\bullet}_k\Lambda_\jmath,a(\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_k)\right\rangle$ for all $\jmath$. Recall that 
\[
\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_k=\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}+n_k\beta^{\imath_\bullet}_k=\mu_{k-1}^{\imath_\bullet}+n_kw^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\gamma_{\imath_k}.
\]
For any $\jmath\neq \imath_k$, we have $w^{\imath_\bullet}_k\Lambda_\jmath=w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_\jmath$, and hence
\[
\left\langle w^{\imath^\bullet}_k\Lambda_\jmath,a(\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_k)\right\rangle=\left\langle w^{\imath^\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_\jmath, a(\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1})\right\rangle+n_k\left\langle \Lambda_\jmath,a(\gamma_{\imath_k})\right\rangle=\left\langle w^{\imath^\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_\jmath, a(\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1})\right\rangle.
\]
In other words, the conditions for $L\in T^{w^{\imath_\bullet}_k}_{\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_k}$ on $\jmath\neq \imath_k$ are identical to those for $L\in T^{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}}_{\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}}$. The remaining condition is associated with $\imath_k$. Note that $s_{\imath_k}\Lambda_{\imath_k}=-\Lambda_{\imath_k}-\sum_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}a_{\jmath\imath_k}\Lambda_\jmath$. Therefore,
\begin{align*}
\left\langle w^{\imath^\bullet}_k\Lambda_{\imath_k},a(\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_k)\right\rangle=&\left\langle w^{\imath^\bullet}_{k-1}s_{\imath_k}\Lambda_{\imath_k},a(\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1})\right\rangle+n_k\left\langle s_{\imath_k}\Lambda_{\imath_k},a(\gamma_{\imath_k})\right\rangle\\
=&-\left\langle w^{\imath^\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{\imath_k},a(\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1})\right\rangle-\sum_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}a_{\jmath\imath_k}\left\langle w^{\imath^\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{\jmath},a(\mu^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1})\right\rangle-n_km_{\imath_k}\\
=&-n_km_{\imath_k}-D_{w_{k-1}^{\imath_\bullet}\Lambda_{\imath_k}}(L)-\sum_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}a_{\jmath \imath_k}D_{w_{k-1}^{\imath_\bullet}\Lambda_{\jmath}}(L).
\end{align*}
This proves the remaining equalities.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
Suppose $W^\tau$ is of type $\mathrm{B}_2$. Fix the reduced word $s_1s_2s_1s_2$ for $w_0$, and let $n_\bullet=(1,2,2,1)$. The coweights $\{\mu_{ k}^{\imath_\bullet}\}_{k=0}^N$ are shown below. The red part is drawn via the other reduced word $s_2s_1s_2s_1$ of $w_0$, where  $n'_\bullet=(3,1,1,4)$ from Proposition \ref{prop:B2'}.
\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.7]
        \node (0) at (0,0) [] {$\bullet$};
        \node (1) at (1,0) [] {$\bullet$};
        \node (2) at (3,2) [] {$\bullet$};
        \node (3) at (3,4) [] {$\bullet$};
        \node (4) at (2,5) [] {$\bullet$};
        \foreach \i in {1,...,4}
        {
        \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\j}{\i-1}
        \draw [->] (\j) -- (\i);
        }
        \node at (0) [below] {$0$};
        \node at (1) [below right] {$\mu_{w_{ 1}^{\imath_\bullet}}$};
        \node at (2) [right] {$\mu_{w_{ 2}^{\imath_\bullet}}$};
        \node at (3) [above right] {$\mu_{w_{ 3}^{\imath_\bullet}}$};
        \node at (4) [above] {$\mu_{w_0}$};
        \node (r1) [red] at (-3,3) [] {$\bullet$};
        \node (r2) [red] at (-3,4) [] {$\bullet$};
        \node (r3) [red] at (-2,5) [] {$\bullet$};
        \draw [->,red] (0) -- (r1);
        \draw [->,red] (r1) -- (r2);
        \draw [->, red] (r2) -- (r3);
        \draw [->, red] (r3) -- (4);
    \end{tikzpicture}
    \caption{Example of an MV polytope.}
\end{figure}
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop: Lusztig transform} Suppose $a_{ij}=a_{ji}=-1$. Then for any non-zero scalars $f_1$, $f_2$, and $f_3$ with $f_1+f_3\neq 0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Lusztig transform}
y_i(f_1)y_j(f_2)y_i(f_3)=y_j\left(\frac{f_2f_3}{f_1+f_3}\right)y_i(f_1+f_3)y_j\left(\frac{f_1f_2}{f_1+f_3}\right)
\end{equation}
Moreover, if $f_1+f_3=0$, then such a transformation does not exist.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem: change of word in special case} Suppose $a_{ij}=a_{ji}=-1$. Then 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)=y_j(1)y_i(f)y_j(-1)y_i(-f)$.
    \item If $f\neq 0$, then for any scalars $f_1$, $f_2$, and $f_3$,
    \[
    y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)\neq y_i(f_1)y_j(f_2)y_i(f_3).
    \]
    \item If $f\neq 0$, $y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)\in N^-\cap Bs_is_js_iB$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
(1) and (2) can be verified by a direct computation in $\operatorname{SL}_3$. Let $i=1$ and $j=2$, then on the one hand,
\[
y_1(1)y_2(-f)y_1(-1)y_2(f)=\begin{pmatrix}
    1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\ f & 0 & 1
\end{pmatrix}=y_2(1)y_1(f)y_2(-1)y_1(-f).
\]
On the other hand,
\[
y_1(f_1)y_2(f_2)y_1(f_3)=\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ f_1+f_3 & 1 & 0 \\ f_2f_3 & f_2 & 1
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
In order to match the $(3,2)$ entry of the two matrices, we need $f_2=0$; but then it forces $f_2f_3=0\neq f$ at the $(3,1)$ entry. Thus, no such $f_1,f_2,f_3$ are possible.

For (3), let $x(f):=x_j(1)x_i(-f)x_j(-1)x_i(f)$. Since $f\neq 0$, there exists some $b\in B$ such that
\[
y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)=x(1/f)\overline{s}_i\overline{s}_j\overline{s}_ib.
\]
This proves that $y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)\in N^-\cap Bs_is_js_iB$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem: limit} Let $\imath$ be an exceptional orbit. In both the $m=2$ and $m=4$ cases in Eq. \eqref{eq: root subgroup element for A2n}, $\lim_{b\rightarrow 0}y_\imath(b,c)=y_\imath(0,c)$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
It suffices to verify the lemma by a computation in $\operatorname{SL}_3$. Let $i=1$ and $j=2$. When $m=2$, we have $a+\gamma(a)=b\gamma(b)$, and hence
\[
\lim_{b\rightarrow 0} y_1\left(\frac{\gamma(a)}{b}\right)y_2(b)y_1\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)=\lim_{b\rightarrow 0}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ \gamma(b) & 1 & 0 \\ a & b & 1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\ a & 0 & 1\end{pmatrix} = y_1(1)y_2(-a)y_1(-1)y_2(a).
\]
When $m=4$, we have $a-\gamma(a)=-t^2b\gamma(b)$, and hence
\begin{align*}
\lim_{b\rightarrow 0} y_1\left(\frac{-\gamma(a)}{tb}\right)y_2(tb)y_1\left(\frac{a}{tb}\right)=\lim_{b\rightarrow 0}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ -t\gamma(b) & 1 & 0 \\ a & tb & 1\end{pmatrix}=&\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\ a & 0 & 1\end{pmatrix}\\ =& y_1(1)y_2(-a)y_1(-1)y_2(a). \qedhere
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop: sigma invariant unipotent} The $y_\imath(a)$ and $y_\imath(b,c)$ in Eq. \eqref{eq: root subgroup elements} and Eq. \eqref{eq: root subgroup element for A2n} are $\sigma$-invariant.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
It is obvious that $y_\imath(a)$ in Eq. \eqref{eq: root subgroup elements} for a standard $\imath$ is $\sigma$-invariant. 

Now we consider the exceptional orbit $\imath=\{l,l+1\}$ in $A_{2l}^{(2)}$. For $m=2$ and $b\neq 0$, we have $a+\gamma(a)=b\gamma(b)$. Hence,
\begin{align*}
\sigma(y_\imath(b,c))=&y_{l+1}\left(\frac{a}{\gamma(b)}\right)y_l\left(\gamma(b)\right)y_{l+1}\left(\frac{\gamma(a)}{\gamma(b)}\right)\\
=&y_l\left(\frac{\gamma(a)}{b}\right)y_{l+1}\left(b\right)y_l\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\\
=&y_\imath(b,c).
\end{align*}
If $b=0$, then $\gamma(a)=-a$. Hence,
\[
\sigma(y_\imath(0,c))=y_{l+1}(1)y_l(a)y_{l+1}(-1)y_l(-a),
\]
which is equal to $y_\imath(0,c)$ by Lemma \ref{lem: change of word in special case} (1).

For $m=4$ and $b\neq 0$, we have $a-\gamma(a)=-t^2b\gamma(b)$, and hence
\begin{align*}
\sigma(y_\imath(b,c))=&y_{l+1}\left(\frac{-a}{t\gamma(b)}\right)y_l\left(-t\gamma(b)\right)y_{l+1}\left(\frac{\gamma(a)}{t\gamma(b)}\right)\\
=&y_l\left(\frac{-\gamma(a)}{tb}\right)y_{l+1}\left(tb\right)y_l\left(\frac{a}{tb}\right)\\
=&y_\imath(b,c).
\end{align*}
If $b=0$, then $\gamma(a)=a$. Hence
\[
\sigma(y_\imath(0,c))=y_{l+1}(\epsilon^{-1})y_l(- \epsilon^{-1}a)y_{l+1}(-\epsilon^{-1})y_l(\epsilon^{-1}a).
\]
By a computation similar to the proof of Lemma \ref{lem: change of word in special case}, one verifies that this is  $y_\imath(0,c)$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:MC-diagram}
For any $g\in G,  w\in W$, and any dominant weight $\Lambda_i$,   we have
\[
\Delta_{\tau(w\Lambda_i)}(\tau(g))=\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g).
\]
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let us set 
\[
f(g)= \langle \xi_{w \Lambda_i},  g \cdot v_{\Lambda_i} \rangle =\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g) \quad \text{and} \quad   f'(g)= \langle  \xi_{\tau(w) \Lambda_{\tau(i)} },  \tau(g)\cdot v_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}} \rangle=\Delta_{\tau(w\Lambda_i)}(\tau(g)).    
\]
Since $f$ and $f'$ are regular functions on $G$, it suffices to check that $f$ and $f'$ agree on the open subset $\overline{w}N^-HN\subset G$.                                                                                              
Recall that $\overline{\tau(w)}=\tau(\overline{w})$ and $\xi_{w\Lambda_i}=\overline{w}\cdot \xi_{\Lambda_i}$. Thus,
for any $y\in N^-, t\in T, x\in N $, on the one hand, 
\[
f(\overline{w}ytx  ) = \langle  \xi_{w\Lambda_i}, \overline{w}ytx\cdot v_{\Lambda_i} \rangle =  \langle  \overline{w}^{-1}\cdot\xi_{w\Lambda_i},  yt \cdot v_{\Lambda_i}   \rangle   =  \Lambda_i(t)  \langle  \xi_{\Lambda_i}, v_{\Lambda_i} \rangle= \Lambda_i(t)  .
\]
On the other hand, 
\begin{align*} f'\left(\overline{w}ytx\right)=&\left\langle\xi_{\tau(w)\Lambda_{\tau(i)}}, \tau(\overline{w})\tau(y)\tau(t)\tau(x)\cdot v_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}}\right\rangle\\
=&\left\langle \xi_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}},\tau(t)\cdot v_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}}\right\rangle\\
=&\Lambda_{\tau(i)}(\tau(t))\langle\xi_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}},v_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}}\rangle\\
=&\Lambda_i(t).
\end{align*}
Thus, $f=f'$ on $G$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem: minor under gamma action} For $g\in G(\mathcal{K})$, $w\in W$, and any dominant weight $\Lambda_i$, we have
\[
\Delta_{\tau(w\Lambda_{i})}(\sigma(g))=\epsilon^{\langle h, w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle}\gamma(\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)).
\]
In particular, $\mathrm{val}(\Delta_{\tau(w\Lambda_i)}(\sigma(g)))=\mathrm{val}(\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g))$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem:MC-diagram}, for any $g\in G(\mathcal{K})$ we have
 $\langle\xi_{\tau(w\Lambda_i)},\tau(g)\cdot v_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}}\rangle=\langle \xi_{w\Lambda_i},g\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle$, where $\tau$ acts on $G$ as a diagram automorphism and acts on $\mathcal{K}$ trivially. On the other hand, we also notice that $\epsilon^h$ is $\tau$-invariant, and hence for $g(f)\in G(\mathcal{K})$,
\begin{align*}
    \Delta_{\tau(w\Lambda_i)}(\sigma(g))=&\langle \xi_{\tau(w\Lambda_i)},\epsilon^h\tau(g(\gamma(f)))\epsilon^{-h}\cdot v_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}}\rangle\\
    =&\epsilon^{\langle h,\tau(w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i)\rangle}\langle \xi_{\tau(w\Lambda_i)},\tau(g(\gamma(f))).v_{\Lambda_{\tau(i)}}\rangle\\
    =&\epsilon^{\langle h,w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle}\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g(\gamma(f)))\\
    =&\epsilon^{\langle h, w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle}\gamma(\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g(f))).\qedhere
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor: gamma invariant minor} Suppose $g\in G(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$, $w\in W^\tau$, and $\tau(i)=i$. Then $\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)$ is $\gamma$-invariant.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem: minor under gamma action}, it suffices to prove that $\epsilon^{\langle h,w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle}=1$. This is obvious if $h=0$. Among the cases with $h\neq 0$, $X_N^{(r)}$ can only be $A_{2l-1}^{(2)}$ and $D_{l+1}^{(2)}$, and $m=2$ for both cases, which implies that $\epsilon=-1$. Note that $w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i$ is an integer linear combination of simple roots. Since both $w$ and $i$ are $\tau$-invariant, the coefficients of $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_{2l-1}$ in $w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i$ must be equal in the case of $A_{2l-1}^{(2)}$, and the coefficients of $\alpha_l$ and $\alpha_{l+1}$ in $w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i$ must be equal in the case of $D_{l+1}^{(2)}$ as well. Thus, it follows that $\langle h,w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle$ must be an even integer and therefore $\epsilon^{\langle h, w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle}=1$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem: pairing mod 4} In the case of $A_{2l}^{(2)}$, let $w\in W^\tau$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\langle h, w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle$ is an even integer for any $\Lambda_i$.
    \item If $i=l,l+1$ and $h=\check{\omega}_l+\check{\omega}_{l+1}$, then $\langle h, w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle\equiv 2M\mod 4$, where $M$ is the number of occurrences of $s_\imath$ for the exceptional $\imath$ in a $\tau$-invariant reduced word of $w\in W^\tau$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Claim (1) is obviously true when $h=0$. When $h=\check{\omega}_l+\check{\omega}_{l+1}$, we need to count the occurrences of $\alpha_l$ and $\alpha_{l+1}$ in $w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i$. Note that $\alpha_l$ and $\alpha_{l+1}$ are only produced during the action of $s_\imath$ for the exceptional $\imath$ in a reduced word of $w$, and for any weight $\mu$,
\begin{align*}
s_ls_{l+1}s_l(\mu)=& s_ls_{l+1}(\mu-\langle \check{\alpha}_l,\mu\rangle\alpha_l)\\
=&s_l(\mu-\langle \check{\alpha}_{l+1},\mu\rangle\alpha_{l+1}-\langle \check{\alpha}_l,\mu\rangle(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1}))\\
=&\mu-\langle \check{\alpha}_l,\mu\rangle \alpha_l-\langle \check{\alpha}_{l+1},\mu\rangle(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1})-\langle \check{\alpha}_l,\mu\rangle\alpha_{l+1}\\
=&\mu-\langle\check{\alpha}_l+\check{\alpha}_{l+1},\mu\rangle(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1}).
\end{align*}
Since $\langle\check{\alpha}_l+\check{\alpha}_{l+1},\mu\rangle$ is always an integer, we can conclude that $w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i$ contains an integer multiple of $\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1}$ in its expansion with respect to simple roots, and hence $\langle h, w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle$ is an even integer, proving claim (1).

For claim (2), we do an induction on $M$. Let $i$ be either $l$ or $l+1$. When $M=0$, neither $\alpha_l$ nor $\alpha_{l+1}$ appears in $w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i$, and therefore $\langle h, w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle=0$. Inductively, we suppose $w=us_\imath v$ for some $u,v\in W^\tau$ such that $v$ does not contain $s_\imath$ in its reduced word; then
\begin{align*}
\langle h, w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle=&\langle h, us_\imath\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle\\
=&\langle h, u(\Lambda_i-\alpha_l-\alpha_{l+1})-\Lambda_i\rangle\\
\equiv &2(M-1)-\langle h, u(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1})\rangle \mod 4.
\end{align*}
Now it remains to prove that $\langle h,u(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1})\rangle\equiv 2\mod 4$. By a similar argument as above, the change in the coefficients of $\alpha_l$ and $\alpha_{l+1}$ in the expansion of $u(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1})$ can only occur as a result of $s_\imath$ for the exceptional $\imath$ in a reduced word of $u$, and the change is equal to $\langle \check{\alpha}_l+\check{\alpha}_{l+1}, \cdot\rangle(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1})$. Since $\langle \alpha_l,\cdot\rangle$ and $\langle \alpha_{l+1},\cdot\rangle$ are equal integers, the change $\langle \check{\alpha}_l+\check{\alpha}_{l+1}, \cdot\rangle(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1})$ paired with $h$ is congruent to $0$ mod $4$. Thus, 
\[
\langle h, u(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1})\rangle\equiv  \langle h, \alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1}\rangle\equiv 2\mod 4.\qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor: A2l with m=4} In the case of $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with $m=4$, if $g\in G(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$ and $w\in W^\tau$, then $\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)\in k(\!(t^2)\!)$ for all $\Lambda_i$.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Note that in this case $\tau^2(i)=i$ and $\langle h,w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle=\langle h, w\Lambda_{\tau(i)}-\Lambda_{\tau(i)}\rangle$. Thus, by Lemmas \ref{lem: minor under gamma action} and \ref{lem: pairing mod 4} (1), we know that
\[
\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)=\Delta_{w\Lambda_{\tau^2(i)}}(g)=\epsilon^{2\langle h,w\Lambda_i-\Lambda_i\rangle} \gamma^2(\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g))=\gamma^2(\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)).
\]
This shows that $\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)$                                                        
must be an element of $k(\!(t^2)\!)$.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor: A2n m=4 middle minor} In the case of $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with $m=4$, if $g\in G(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$ and $w\in W^\tau$, then $\Delta_{ws_l\Lambda_l}(g)$ and $\Delta_{ws_{l+1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)$ are in $tk(\!(t^2)\!)$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Let $w'=ws_ls_{l+1}s_l=ws_{l+1}s_ls_{l+1}\in W^\tau$. By Lemma \ref{lem: minor under gamma action} and Corollary \ref{cor: A2l with m=4}, we know that 
\begin{align*}\Delta_{ws_{l+1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)=&\Delta_{\tau(ws_l\Lambda_l)}(\sigma(g))=\epsilon^{\langle h,ws_l\Lambda_l-\Lambda_l\rangle}\gamma(\Delta_{ws_l\Lambda_l}(g)),\\
\Delta_{w\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)=&\Delta_{\tau(w\Lambda_l)}(\sigma(g))=\epsilon^{\langle h, w\Lambda_l-\Lambda_l\rangle}\gamma(\Delta_{w\Lambda_l}(g)),\\
\Delta_{w'\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)=&\Delta_{\tau(w'\Lambda_l)}(\sigma(g))=\epsilon^{\langle h, w'\Lambda_l-\Lambda_l\rangle}\gamma(\Delta_{w'\Lambda_l}(g)).
\end{align*}
We also observe that
\[
\langle h,ws_l\Lambda_l-w\Lambda_l\rangle=-\langle h, w\alpha_l\rangle \quad \text{and} \quad \langle h,w'\Lambda_l-w\Lambda_l\rangle=-\langle h, w(\alpha_l+\alpha_{l+1})\rangle=-2\langle h, w\alpha_l\rangle.
\]
Now let us consider the following identity of generalized minors (\cite[Proposition 4.2]{BZ})
\begin{equation}\label{eq: minor identity}
\Delta_{ws_{l+1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{ws_l\Lambda_l}(g)=\Delta_{w\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w'\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)+\Delta_{w\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w'\Lambda_l}(g).
\end{equation}
With appropriate substitution and cancellation, we get
\begin{align*}
\Delta_{ws_l\Lambda_l}(g)\gamma(\Delta_{ws_l\Lambda_l}(g))=&\epsilon^{-\langle h, w\alpha_l\rangle}\Delta_{w\Lambda_l}(g)\gamma(\Delta_{w'\Lambda_l}(g))+\epsilon^{\langle h, w\alpha_l\rangle}\gamma(\Delta_{w\Lambda_l}(g))\Delta_{w'\Lambda_l}(g)\\
=&\epsilon^{\langle h, w\alpha_l\rangle}\left(\gamma(\Delta_{w\Lambda_l}(g))\Delta_{w'\Lambda_l}(g)-\Delta_{w\Lambda_l}(g)\gamma(\Delta_{w'\Lambda_l}(g))\right).
\end{align*}
Since both $\gamma(\Delta_{w\Lambda_l}(g))\Delta_{w'\Lambda_l}(g)$ and $\Delta_{w\Lambda_l}(g)\gamma(\Delta_{w'\Lambda_l}(g))$ are in $k(\!(t^2)\!)$ and are $\gamma$-images of each other, we can conclude that the right hand side of the above equation are in $t^2k(\!(t^4)\!)$. This implies that $\Delta_{ws_l\Lambda_l}(g)$, and hence $\Delta_{ws_{l+1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)$, must be in $tk(\!(t^2)\!)$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{prop: quotient in K} Suppose $\imath$ is a standard orbit and $i\in \imath$. Suppose $g\in G(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$ and $w\in W^\tau$ such that $\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)$ and $\Delta_{ws_\imath\Lambda_i}(g)$ are both non-zero. Then
\[
\frac{\prod_{k\neq i}\Delta_{w\Lambda_k}(g)^{-a_{ki}}}{\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)\Delta_{ws_\imath\Lambda_i}(g)}\in \mathcal{K}_{\imath}.
\]
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
If $m_{\imath}=m/|\imath|=1$, there is nothing to show. The standard orbits in the case $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with $m=4$ follows from Corollary \ref{cor: A2l with m=4}. Thus, it remains to consider $\tau$-fixed points, i.e., $\imath=\{i\}$. Note that by Corollary \ref{cor: gamma invariant minor}, both $\Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g)$ and $\Delta_{ws_\imath\Lambda_i}(g)$ are $\gamma$-invariant. 

On the one hand, if $h=0$, by Lemma \ref{lem: minor under gamma action}, the product $\prod_{k\neq i}\Delta_{w\Lambda_k}(g)^{-a_{ki}}$ is obviously $\gamma$-invariant as well, and therefore the whole quotient must be in the $\gamma$-invariant subfield of $\mathcal{K}$, which is $\mathcal{K}_{\imath}$. 

On the other hand, among the cases with $h\neq 0$, the only cases with $\tau$-fixed indices are $A_{2l-1}^{(2)}$ and $D_{l+1}^{(2)}$. Now for $k\neq i$, either $\tau(k)=k$ and hence $\Delta_{w\Lambda_k}(g)^{-a_{ki}}$ is $\gamma$-invariant by Corollary \ref{cor: gamma invariant minor}, or $\{k,k'\}$ is a $\tau$-orbit and both $\Delta_{w\Lambda_k}(g)$ and $\Delta_{w\Lambda_{k'}}(g)=\epsilon^{\langle h,w\Lambda_k-\Lambda_k\rangle}\gamma(\Delta_{w\Lambda_k}(g))$ (Lemma \ref{lem: minor under gamma action}) are present in the product with the same power $-a_{ki}$, which implies that the product is $\gamma$-invariant. Thus, the whole quotient must again be in $\mathcal{K}_{\imath}$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem: representative}
For any $g\in N^-(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$, there exists $h\in (N^-)^\sigma$, such that

\[ {\rm val}(  \Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g \cdot h) )=D_{w\Lambda_\imath}(g) \quad \text{ for any $w\in W^\tau$ and any $\imath\in I_\tau$}  .  \]
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
We adapt part of the proof of \cite[Theorem 4.5]{Kam} into our setting.

Let $\overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)'$ be the span of all $n\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}, n\in (N^-)^\sigma$.  Then $\overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)'$ is a rational $(N^-)^\sigma$-submodule of $\overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)$. Hence, it is stable under the action of the hyperalgebra $\mathfrak{D}((N^-)^\sigma)$ of $(N^{-})^\sigma$.  Since $\overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)$ is a Weyl module with $v_{\Lambda_i}$ as a highest weight vector,  by \cite[I.7.15, II.2.13]{Ja} we have $\mathfrak{D}((N^-)^\sigma)\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}=\overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)$. Thus, $\overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)'=\overline{V}(\Lambda_\imath)$.


Choose a basis $\{u_s\}$ in $\overline{V}(\Lambda_\iota)$ (as a subspace of $V(\Lambda_i)$). Let $\{u_s^*\}$ be the dual basis of $\{u_s\}$ in $\overline{V}(\Lambda_\iota)^*$ (as a quotient of $V(\Lambda_i)^*$).  
For any $h\in (N^-)^\sigma$,  
\[ h\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}=\sum_{s} c_s u_s \in  \overline{V}(\Lambda_\iota) .\]
Set $d=D_{w\Lambda_i}(g)$. Let $p_s$ be the coefficient of $t^{d}$ in $\langle \xi_{w\Lambda}, g\cdot u_s      \rangle$.  Since 
\[ D_{w\Lambda_i}(g)= \min_{s} \{ \mathrm{val} ( \langle \xi_{w\Lambda_i}, g\cdot u_s   \rangle  ) \} , \]
there exists $s$ such that $p_s$ is nonzero. Set $u^*= \sum_s p_s u_s^* \in \overline{V}(\Lambda_i)^*$.  
 Then, $u^*$ is nonzero, and ${\rm val}(  \Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g \cdot h) )=d$ if and only if $\langle u^*, h\cdot v_{\Lambda_i} \rangle =\sum_s p_s c_s$ is nonzero.  

 For each $w\Lambda_{\iota}$ (with a choice of $i\in \iota$), we define a function $f_{w\Lambda_\iota}:  (N^-)^\sigma\to {\mathrm{k}}$ with $f_{w\Lambda_\iota}(h)=\langle u^*, h\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}  \rangle$.
Then, $f_{w\Lambda_\iota}$ is nonzero regular function on $(N^-)^\sigma$.  Since $(N^-)^\sigma$ is an irreducible variety, we can find an element $h\in (N^-)^\sigma$ such that $f_{w\Lambda_{\iota}}(h)\not= 0$ for all $w\Lambda_{\iota}$. Thus, there exists $h\in (N^-)^\sigma$, such that for any $w\Lambda_\iota$,
 \[ {\rm val}(  \Delta_{w\Lambda_i}(g \cdot h) )=D_{w\Lambda_\imath}(g) . \qedhere\]
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
For convenience, we denote flags by the symbol ``$B$'' and decorated flags by the symbol ``$A$''. We use subscripts for left cosets of $B$ and $N$, and superscripts for right cosets of $B^-$ and $N^-$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem: relative position} Suppose $w=uv$ in $W$ and $l(w)=l(u)+l(v)$. Then
\begin{enumerate}\item $B_1\xrightarrow{w}B_3$ if and only if there exists $B_2$ such that $B_1\xrightarrow{u}B_2\xrightarrow{v} B_3$. Moreover, such $B_2$ is unique if it exists.
\item $A_1\ext@arrow 0359\Rightarrowfill@{}{w}A_3$ if and only if there exists $A_2$ such that $A_1\ext@arrow 0359\Rightarrowfill@{}{u}A_2\ext@arrow 0359\Rightarrowfill@{}{v} A_3$. Moreover, such $A_2$ is unique if it exists.
\item $B^1\overset{u}{\mbox{ --- }}B_3$ if and only if there exists $B_2$ such that $B^1\overset{w}{\mbox{ --- }}B_2\xrightarrow{v^{-1}}B_3$. Moreover, such $B_2$ is unique if it exists.
\item $B^1\overset{v}{\mbox{ --- }}B_3$ if and only if there exists $B^2$ such that $B^1\xrightarrow{u^{-1}}B^2\overset{w}{\mbox{ --- }}B_3$. Moreover, such $B^2$ is unique if it exists.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem: possible Tits codistance} 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Suppose $w\in W$ with $l(ws_i)>l(w)$. If $B^1\overset{w}{\mbox{ --- }}B_2\xrightarrow{s_i}B_3$, then the Tits codistance from $B^1$ to $B_3$ is either $w$ or $ws_i$.
    \item Suppose $w\in W$ with $l(s_iw)>l(w)$. If $B^1\overset{w}{\mbox{ --- }}B_2\xleftarrow{s_i}B_3$, then the Tits codistance from $B^1$ to $B_3$ is either $w$ or $s_iw$.
    \item If $B_1\xrightarrow{s_i}B_2\xrightarrow{s_i}B_3$, then the Tits distance from $B_1$ to $B_3$ is either $e$ or $s_i$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The first two claims are proved in {\cite[Proposition A.8]{ShenWeng}}. The last claim follows from the fact that $Bs_iBs_iB=B\sqcup Bs_iB$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
A \emph{configuration} of flags is a tuple of (decorated) flags satisfying certain Tits (co)distance and compatibility conditions, considered up to a diagonal $G$-action.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem: Gaussian decomposable} An element $g\in G$ is Gaussian decomposable if and only if $\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(g)\neq 0$ for all $\Lambda_i$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem: Gaussian decomposition} Suppose $g\in G$ is Gaussian decomposable and $\sigma(g)=g$. Then $\sigma([g]_-)=[g]_-$, $\sigma([g]_0)=[g]_0$, and $\sigma([g]_+)=[g]_+$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Since $\sigma$ preserves $N^-$, $H$, and $N$, $\sigma([g]_-)\sigma([g]_0)\sigma([g]_+)$ is a Gaussian decomposition of $\sigma(g)=g$. The statement then follows from the uniqueness of the Gaussian decomposition.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem: in N^-(w_0)} An element $g\in N^-$ is in $N^-(w_0)$ if and only if $\Delta_{w_0\Lambda_i}(g)\neq 0$ for all $\Lambda_i$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Note that 
\[
\Delta_{w_0\Lambda_i}(g)=\langle \xi_{w_0\Lambda_i},g\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\langle \overline{w}_0\cdot \xi_{\Lambda_i},g\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, \overline{w}_0^{-1}g\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\Delta_i(\overline{w}_0^{-1}g).
\]
Thus, by Lemma \ref{lem: Gaussian decomposable}, $\Delta_{w_0\Lambda_i}(g)\neq 0$ for all $\Lambda_i$ implies that $\overline{w}_0^{-1}g\in B^{-1}B$, which is equivalent to $g\in (w_0B^-w_0)w_0B=Bw_0B$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem: Lusztig variables in terms of A variables} Suppose $\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.6,baseline=5] \node (1) at (0,0) [] {$B_1$};
\node (2) at (1,2) [] {$A^2$};
\node (3) at (2,0) [] {$B_3$};
\draw (1) -- (2);
\draw (2) -- (3);
\draw [->] (1) -- node [below] {$s_i$} (3);
\end{tikzpicture}$. Let $A_1\ext@arrow 0359\Rightarrowfill@{}{s_i}A_3$ be compatible lifts of $B_1\xrightarrow{s_i}B_3$. Let $\Delta_j:=\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(A^2,A_1)$ and $\Delta'_j:=\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(A^2,A_3)$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\Delta_j=\Delta'_j$ for $j\neq i$.
    \item $u(B_1,A^2,B_3) = y_i(f)$ for
    \[
    f=\frac{\prod_{j\neq i} \Delta_j^{-a_{ji}}}{\Delta_i\Delta'_i}
    \]
    where $a_{ji}$ is the Cartan matrix of $G$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop: vanishing minor implies special angle unipotent} Suppose $a_{ij}=a_{ji}=-1$ in the Cartan matrix and suppose $\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.6,baseline=5] \node (1) at (0,0) [] {$B_1$};
\node (2) at (1.5,2) [] {$A^2$};
\node (3) at (3,0) [] {$B_3$};
\draw (1) -- (2);
\draw (2) -- (3);
\draw [->] (1) -- node [below] {$s_is_js_i$} (3);
\end{tikzpicture}$. Let $A_1\ext@arrow 0359\Rightarrowfill@{}{s_is_js_i}A_3$ be compatible lifts of $B_1\xrightarrow{s_is_js_i}B_3$, and let $A_4$, $A_5$, $A_6$, and $A_7$ be decorated flags that fit into the diagram below (the existence and uniqueness of these flags follow from Lemma \ref{lem: relative position}).
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}
    \node (1) at (0,0) [] {$A_1$};
    \node (4) at (1.5,0.5) [] {$A_4$};
    \node (5) at (3,0.5) [] {$A_5$};
    \node (3) at (4.5,0) [] {$A_3$};
    \node (6) at (1.5,-0.5) [] {$A_6$};
    \node (7) at (3,-0.5) [] {$A_7$};
    \draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (1) -- node [above left] {$s_i$} (4);
    \draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (4) -- node [above] {$s_j$} (5);
    \draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (5) -- node [above right] {$s_i$} (3);
    \draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (1) -- node [below left] {$s_j$} (6);
    \draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (6) -- node [below] {$s_i$} (7);
    \draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (7) -- node [below right] {$s_j$} (3);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
Then the following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $u(B_1,A^2,B_3)=y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)$ for some $f\neq 0$.
    \item $\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(A^2,A_4)=\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(A^2,A_5)=\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(A^2,A_6)=\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(A^2,A_7)=0$.
    \item At least one of $\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(A^2,A_4)$ and $\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(A^2,A_5)$ vanishes, and at least one of $\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(A^2,A_6)$ and $\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(A^2,A_7)$ vanishes.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
(1)$\implies$(2). By Lemma \ref{lem: possible Tits codistance}, the Tits codistances from $A^2$ to $A_4$ and $A_5$, respectively, can only be both $e$ or both $s_i$. We claim that they cannot be $e$; otherwise, by Lemma \ref{lem: Lusztig variables in terms of A variables}, there must exist non-zero scalars $f_1$, $f_2$, and $f_3$ such that $u(B_1,A^2,B_4)=y_i(f_1)$, $u(B_4,A^2,B_5)=y_j(f_2)$, and $u(B_5,A^2,B_3)=y_i(f_3)$ (here $B_4$ and $B_5$ are the underlying undecorated flags of $A_4$ and $A_5$). This implies that
\[
y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)=u(B_1, A^2,B_3)=y_i(f_1)y_j(f_2)y_i(f_3),
\]
which is impossible by Lemma \ref{lem: change of word in special case} (2).

Now given that $A^2\overset{s_i}{\mbox{ --- }}A_4$, we can act diagonally by some element of $G$ and assume that $(A_1,A^2,A_4)=(tN, N^-,t\overline{s}_i^{-1}N)$. This implies that
\[
\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(A^2,A_4)=\Delta_{\Lambda_i}\left(t\overline{s}_i^{-1}\right)=\Lambda_i(t)\langle \overline{s}_i\cdot \xi_{\Lambda_i}, v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\Lambda_i(t)\langle \xi_{s_i\Lambda_i},v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=0.
\]
The vanishing of the other three functions can be proved similarly as well. 

(2)$\implies$(3) is obvious.

(3)$\implies$(1). By the same argument as in the proof of (1)$\implies$(2) part, we know that when (3) is true, we must have the following Tits codistances as shown in the left picture of Figure \ref{fig: special unipotent computation}.
\begin{figure}[H]
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.3]
    \node (1) at (0,0) [] {$B_1$};
    \node (4) at (1.5,0.8) [] {$B_4$};
    \node (5) at (3,0.8) [] {$B_5$};
    \node (3) at (4.5,0) [] {$B_3$};
    \node (6) at (1.5,-0.8) [] {$B_6$};
    \node (7) at (3,-0.8) [] {$B_7$};
    \draw [->] (1) -- node [above] {$s_i$} (4);
    \draw [->] (4) -- node [below] {$s_j$} (5);
    \draw [->] (5) -- node [above] {$s_i$} (3);
    \draw [->] (1) -- node [below left] {$s_j$} (6);
    \draw [->] (6) -- node [below] {$s_i$} (7);
    \draw [->] (7) -- node [below right] {$s_i$} (3);
    \node (2) at (2.25,3.5) [] {$A^2$};
    \draw (1) -- (2) -- node [below left] {$s_i$} (4);
    \draw (2) -- node [below] {\quad$s_j$}(6);
    \draw (2) -- node [below] {$s_j$\quad\quad} (7);
    \draw (5) -- node [below right] {$s_i$} (2) -- (3);
\end{tikzpicture}\hspace{1cm}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.3]
    \node (1) at (0,0) [] {$B_1$};
    \node (4) at (1.5,0.8) [] {$B_4$};
    \node (5) at (3,0.8) [] {$B_5$};
    \node (3) at (4.5,0) [] {$B_3$};
    \node (6) at (1.5,-0.8) [] {$B_6$};
    \node (7) at (3,-0.8) [] {$B_7$};
    \draw [->] (1) -- node [above] {$s_i$} (4);
    \draw [->] (4) -- node [below] {$s_j$} (5);
    \draw [->] (5) -- node [above] {$s_i$} (3);
    \draw [->] (1) -- node [below left] {$s_j$} (6);
    \draw [->] (6) -- node [below] {$s_i$} (7);
    \draw [->] (7) -- node [below right] {$s_i$} (3);
    \node (2) at (2.25,3.5) [] {$A^2$};
    \draw (1) -- (2) -- node [below left] {$s_i$} (4);
    \draw (2) -- node [below] {\quad$s_j$}(6);
    \draw (2) -- node [below] {$s_j$\quad\quad} (7);
    \draw (5) -- node [below right] {$s_i$} (2) -- (3);
    \node [blue] (8) at (1,0) [] {$B_8$};
    \draw [->,blue] (1) -- node [below right] {$s_i$} (8);
    \draw [blue] (8) -- (2);
    \draw [blue,->] (8) -- node [right] {$s_i$} (4);
    \node [blue] (9) at (2.25,0) [] {$B_9$};
    \node [blue] (10) at (3.5,0) [] {$B_{10}$};
    \draw [blue,->] (8) -- node [below] {$s_j$} (9);
    \draw [blue,->] (9) -- node [above] {$s_i$} (10);
    \draw [blue,->] (10) --node [above left] {$s_j$} (3);
    \draw [blue,->] (10) -- node [left] {$s_j$} (7);
    \draw [blue] (9) -- (2) -- (10);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Illustration of the step (3)$\implies$(1).}\label{fig: special unipotent computation}
\end{figure}
Now let $B_8$ be the unique flag with $A^2\mbox{ --- }B_8$ and $u(B_1,A^2,B_8)=y_i(1)$. Note that $B_8\neq B_4$ because $A^2\overset{s_i}{\mbox{ --- }}B_4$; then by Lemma \ref{lem: possible Tits codistance} (3), we can deduce that $B_8\xrightarrow{s_i}B_4$. Then by Lemma \ref{lem: relative position} (1), we know that $B_8\xrightarrow{s_is_js_i}B_3$. Now let $B_9$ and $B_{10}$ be the unique flags that satisfy $B_8\xrightarrow{s_j}B_9\xrightarrow{s_i}B_{10}\xrightarrow{s_j}B_3$. 

We claim that the Tits codistance from $A^2$ to either of $B_9$ and $B_{10}$ is $e$. To see this, suppose $A^2\overset{s_j}{\mbox{ --- }}B_{10}$. Then by Lemma \ref{lem: relative position} (3), we have $B_{10}=B_7$; but then the Tits distance from $B_1$ to $B_{10}=B_7$ is $s_is_js_i$ if we go through $B_8$ and $B_9$, and is $s_js_i$ if we go through $B_6$, which is absurd. Thus, we must have $A^2\mbox{ --- }B_{10}$ and hence $A^2\mbox{ --- }B_9$ as well.

Since the chain $B_8\xrightarrow{s_j}B_9\xrightarrow{s_i} B_{10}\xrightarrow{s_j}B_3$ cannot be switched to a chain with reduced word $s_is_js_i$ while maintaining the general position between $A^2$ and the intermediate flags, we know that the angle unipotents $u(B_8,A^2,B_9)=y_j(-f)$ and $u(B_{10},A^2,B_3)=y_j(f)$ for some non-zero scalar $f$ (Proposition \ref{prop: Lusztig transform}).

Lastly, by the same argument as for the Tits distance from $B_8$ to $B_4$, we know that $B_7\xrightarrow{s_j}B_{10}$. But then we observe that the chain $B_1\xrightarrow{s_i}B_8\xrightarrow{s_j}B_9\xrightarrow{s_i}B_{10}$ cannot be switched to a chain with reduced word $s_js_is_j$ while maintaining the general position between $A^2$ and the intermediate flags. Thus, $u(B_9,A^2,B_{10})=u(B_1,A^2,B_8)^{-1}=y_i(-1)$. By multiplying the angle unipotents together, we can conclude that
\begin{align*}
u(B_1,A^2,B_3)=&u(B_1,A^2,B_8)\cdot u(B_8,A^2,B_9)\cdot u(B_9,A^2,B_{10})\cdot u(B_{10},A^2,B_3)\\
=&y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)
\end{align*}
for some scalar $f\neq 0$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem: special case} Suppose $a_{ij}=a_{ji}=-1$ in the Cartan matrix and suppose $\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.6,baseline=5] \node (1) at (0,0) [] {$B_1$};
\node (2) at (1.5,2) [] {$A^2$};
\node (3) at (3,0) [] {$B_3$};
\draw (1) -- (2);
\draw (2) -- (3);
\draw [->] (1) -- node [below] {$s_is_js_i$} (3);
\end{tikzpicture}$ with
\[
u(B_1,A^2,B_3)=y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)
\]
for some non-zero scalar $f$. Let $A_1\ext@arrow 0359\Rightarrowfill@{}{s_is_js_i}A_3$ be compatible lifts of $B_1\xrightarrow{s_is_js_i}B_3$. Let $\Delta_j:=\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(A^2,A_1)$ and $\Delta'_j:=\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(A^2,A_3)$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\Delta_k=\Delta'_k$ for $k\neq i,j$.
    \item $f=\dfrac{\prod_{k\neq i,j}\Delta_k^{-a_{ki}-a_{kj}}}{\Delta_j\Delta'_i}=-\dfrac{\prod_{k\neq i,j}\Delta_k^{-a_{ki}-a_{kj}}}{\Delta_i\Delta'_j}$.
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Let $y(f)=y_i(1)y_j(-f)y_i(-1)y_j(f)$ and $x(f)=x_j(1)x_i(-f)x_j(-1)x_i(f)$. Then there exists some $n\in N$ such that
\[
x(1/f)\overline{s}_i\overline{s}_j\overline{s}_i=y(f)\cdot \check{\alpha}_i(1/f)\cdot\check{\alpha}_j(-1/f) \cdot n.
\]
Without loss of generality, we may assume that 
\[
(A_1,A^2,A_3)=(tN, N^-,x(1/f)\overline{s}_i\overline{s}_j\overline{s}_it'N),
\]
with $t'=s_is_js_i(t)$. Note that $\Delta_k=\Lambda_k(t)$ and $\Delta'_k=\Lambda_k\left(\check{\alpha}_i(1/f)\cdot\check{\alpha}_j(-1/f) \cdot t'\right)$

Now for $k\neq i,j$,
\[
\Delta_k=\Lambda_k(t)=\Lambda_k(s_is_js_i(t))=\Lambda_k(t')=\Lambda_k\left(\check{\alpha}_i(1/f)\cdot\check{\alpha}_j(-1/f) \cdot t'\right)=\Delta'_k,
\]
proving claim (1). 

For claim (2), note that
\[
\Delta'_i=\Lambda_i\left(\check{\alpha}_i(1/f)\cdot\check{\alpha}_j(-1/f) \cdot t'\right)=\frac{1}{f}\Lambda_i(s_is_js_i(t))=\frac{1}{f}\Lambda_i(t)\alpha_i(t^{-1})\alpha_j(t^{-1}).
\]
In total, we have
\[
\frac{\prod_{k\neq i,j}\Delta_k^{-a_{ki}-a_{kj}}}{\Delta_j\Delta'_i}=\frac{\prod_{k\neq i,j} \Lambda_k(t)^{-a_{ki}-a_{kj}}}{\frac{1}{f}\Lambda_j(t)\Lambda_i(t)\alpha_i(t^{-1})\alpha_j(t^{-1})}=f,
\]
where the last equality follows from the fact that 
\begin{align*}
\alpha_i+\alpha_j-\Lambda_i-\Lambda_j-\sum_{k\neq i,j}(a_{ki}+a_{kj})\Lambda_k=&\left(\sum_k(a_{ki}+a_{kj})\Lambda_k\right)-\Lambda_i-\Lambda_j-\sum_{k\neq i,j}(a_{ki}+a_{kj})\Lambda_k\\=&0.
\end{align*}

The identity $f=-\dfrac{\prod_{k\neq i,j}\Delta_k^{-a_{ki}-a_{kj}}}{\Delta_i\Delta'_j}$ in (2) then follows by first applying Lemma \ref{lem: change of word in special case} (1) to $u(B_1,A^2,B_3)$ and then doing the same proof with $i$ and $j$ switched.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
We define an anti-involution $t:g\mapsto g^t$ on $G$ called the \emph{(modified) transposition}. It is defined on the generators of $G$ by
\begin{align*}
x_i(f)^t:=&\left\{\begin{array}{ll} y_i(f) & \text{if $\left(X_N^{(r)},m,i\right)\neq \left(A_{2l}^{(2)},4,l\right)$},\\
y_i(-f) & \text{if $\left(X_N^{(r)},m,i\right)= \left(A_{2l}^{(2)},4,l\right)$},\end{array}\right.\\
h^t:=&h \quad \text{for all $h\in H$}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
Note that except for the case $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with $m=4$, our modified transposition coincides with the usual transposition on $G$. Thus, we will drop the word ``modified'' and refer to $g^t$ simply as the ``transposition'' of $g$.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem: transposition of Weyl group element lift} For each Weyl group element $w$, there exists some element $h_w\in H(k)$ such that $\overline{w}^t=h_w\overline{w}^{-1}$. Moreover, if any cases other than $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with $m=4$, $h_w=e$ for all $w$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
On the one hand, when $\left(X_N^{(r)},m,i\right)\neq \left(A_{2l}^{(2)},4,l\right)$, we have
\[
\overline{s}_i^t=\left(x_i(-1)y_i(1)x_i(-1)\right)^t=y_i(-1)x_i(1)y_i(-1)=\overline{s}_i^{-1}.
\]
On the other hand, when $\left(X_N^{(r)},m,i\right)=\left(A_{2l}^{(2)},4,l\right)$, we have
\[
\overline{s}_l^t=y_l(1)x_l(-1)y_l(1)=\overline{s}_l=\check{\alpha}_l(-1)\overline{s}_l^{-1}.
\]
Let $(i_1,i_2,\dots, i_k)$ be a reduced word for $w$. Then
\[
\overline{w}^t=\overline{s}_{i_l}^t\overline{s}_{i_{l-1}}^t\cdots \overline{s}_{i_1}^t=h_w\overline{s}_{i_l}^{-1}\overline{s}_{i_{l-1}}^{-1}\cdots \overline{s}_{i_1}^{-1}=h_w\overline{w}^{-1},
\]
where $h_w$ is obtained by pushing the possible factors of the form $\check{\alpha}_l(-1)$ all the way to the left.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
The Tits (co)distances satisfy the following properties under transposition:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $B_1\xrightarrow{w}B_2$ if and only if $(B_2)^t\xrightarrow{w^{-1}}(B_1)^t$;
    \item $B^1 \mbox{ --- } B_1$ if and only if $(B_1)^t  \mbox{ --- } (B^1)^t$;
    \item $A^1 \mathrel{\leaders\hbox to3pt{$=$\hss}\hskip10pt=} A_1$ if and only if $(A_1)^t\mathrel{\leaders\hbox to3pt{$=$\hss}\hskip10pt=}(A^1)^t$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
These follow from the fact that $\overline{w}^t$ is a lift of $w^{-1}$, and transposition swaps $B$ with $B^-$, and swaps $N$ with $N^-$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem: transposition} The automorphism $\sigma$ commutes with the transposition $t$.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
This identity is obviously true on $H$. Thus, it suffices to verify it on the generators $x_i(f)$ and $y_i(f)$. When $m=2$, we have $\epsilon=-1$ and therefore
\begin{align*}
\sigma(x_i(f)^t)=\sigma(y_i(f))=&y_{\tau(i)}\left((-1)^{-\alpha_i(h)}\gamma(f)\right)=y_{\tau(i)}\left((-1)^{\alpha_i(h)}\gamma(f)\right)\\
=&x_{\tau(i)}\left((-1)^{\alpha_i(h)}\gamma(f)\right)^t=\left(\sigma(x_i(f))\right)^t.
\end{align*}

When $m=3$, $h=0$ by Eq. \eqref{eq_h}, and therefore $\alpha_i(h)=0$ for all $i$. It then follows that
\[
\sigma(x_i(f)^t)=\sigma(y_i(f))=y_{\tau(i)}(\gamma(f))=x_{\tau(i)}(\gamma(f))^t=(\sigma(x_i(f)))^t.
\]

When $m=4$, $\alpha_i(h)=0$ for all $i$ except $i=l,l+1$. Thus, for $i\neq l,l+1$, we can show $\sigma(x_i(f))^t=(\sigma(x_i(f)))^t$ in the same way as the $m=3$ case above. For $i=l$, we have
\[
\sigma(x_l(f)^t)=\sigma(y_l(-f))=y_{l+1}(-\epsilon^{-1}\gamma(f))=x_{l+1}(\epsilon\gamma(f))^t=(\sigma(x_l(f)))^t,
\]
and for $i=l+1$, we have
\[
\sigma(x_{l+1}(f)^t)=\sigma(y_{l+1}(f))=y_{l}(\epsilon^{-1}\gamma(f))=x_l(\epsilon\gamma(f))^t=(\sigma(x_{l+1}(f)))^t.
\]

Similar computations prove that $\sigma(y_i(f)^t)=\left(\sigma(y_i(f))\right)^t$ in all cases as well.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop: rotation} Suppose we have the following configuration of a quadruple of flags associated with $G$.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.2]
    \node (1) at (2,2) [] {$A^1$};
    \node (2) at (2,0) [] {$A_2$};
    \node (3) at (0,0) [] {$B_3$};
    \node (4) at (0,2) [] {$B^4$};
\draw [double, double distance=1pt] (2) -- (1);
    \draw [->] (3) -- node [below] {$w$} (2);
    \draw [->] (4) -- node [above] {$w^{-1}$} (1);
    \draw (3) -- node [left] {$w^{-1}$} (4);
    \draw (1) -- (3);
    \draw (2) -- (4);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
Let $g:=u(B_3,A^1,B_2)$ and $y:=u\left((B^1)^t, (A_2)^t, (B^4)^t\right)$. Then 
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $B^4$ is uniquely determined by the pair $(B^1,B_3)$.
  \item $B_3$ is uniquely determined by the pair $(B^4, B_2)$.
    \item both $y$ and $g$ are in $N^-(w)$ and they are related by
\[
y=[g^tw]_- \quad \text{and} \quad g=\left([wy^{-1}]_+^t\right)^{-1}.
\]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
(1) and (2) are proved in \cite[Lemma 2.5]{ShenWeng}. For (3), to prove $y=[g^tw]_-$, without loss of generality, let us start with the configuration 
\[
(A^1,A_2,B_3,B^4) = (N^-, gN, B, B^-w^{-1}).
\]
It is clear that $u(B_3,A^1,B_2)=g$. After taking transposition, we see that $((B^1)^t, (A_2)^t, (B^4)^t)=(B, N^-g^t, wB)$; then we apply the action by $g^t$ and get
\[
g^t\cdot ((B^1)^t, (A_2)^t, (B^4)^t)=(B,N^-,g^twB),
\]
which yields $u((B^1)^t, (A_2)^t, (B^4)^t)=[g^tw]_-$.

Conversely, to prove $g=\left([wy^{-1}]^t_+\right)^{-1}$, we start with the configuration
\[
(A^1,A_2,B_3,B^4)=\left(N^-(y^t)^{-1},N,w^{-1}B,B^-\right).
\]
It is clear that $u\left((B^1)^t, (A_2)^t,(B^4)^t\right)=y$. Now by acting on $(B_3,A^1,B_2)=(w^{-1}B,N^-(y^t)^{-1},B)$ by $(y^t)^{-1}$, we get
\[
(y^t)^{-1}\cdot (B_3,A^1,B_2)=((y^t)^{-1}w^{-1}B,N^-,B).
\]
Thus,
\[
g:=u(B_3,A^1,B_2)=\left[(y^t)^{-1}w^{-1}\right]_-^{-1}=\left[(wy^{-1})^t\right]_-^{-1}=\left([wy^{-1}]_+^t\right)^{-1}. \qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
The expressions $\left[g^tw\right]_-$ and $[wy^{-1}]_+$ are well-defined because different lifts of $w$ differ only by multiplication by an element of $H$ on the left or right, which does not affect the corresponding Gaussian decomposition.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
The \emph{twist map} on $N^-(w)$ is defined by
\begin{align*}
    \eta_w:N^-(w)&\longrightarrow N^-(w)\\
    g & \longmapsto y=[g^tw]_-.
\end{align*}
Its \emph{inverse twist map} is then defined by
\begin{align*}
    \eta_w^{-1}:N^-(w)&\longrightarrow N^-(w)\\
    y & \longmapsto g=\left([wy^{-1}]_+^t\right)^{-1}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem: sigma invariant} If $g,y\in N^-(w)(\mathcal{K})$ satisfy $y=\eta_w(g)$, then $\sigma(g)=g$ if and only if $\sigma(y)=y$.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
This follows from the fact that the automorphism $\sigma$ commutes with group inverse (since it is a group automorphism), transposition (Lemma \ref{lem: transposition}), and Gaussian decomposition (Lemma \ref{lem: Gaussian decomposition}).
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop: minor recursive formula} Let $i_\bullet=(i_1,i_2,\dots, i_m)$ be a reduced word of $w$. Let $a_1,a_2,\dots, a_m$ be elements in $\mathcal{K}$ and set
\[
y=y_{i_1}(a_1) y_{i_2}(a_2)\cdots y_{i_m}(a_m)\in N^-(w).
\]
Let $g:=\eta_w^{-1}(y)$. For $0\leq k\leq m$, define $w^{i_\bullet}_k:=s_{i_1}s_{i_2}\cdots s_{i_k}$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
    \item 
    \[a_k=\lambda^{i_\bullet}_k\dfrac{\prod_{j\neq i_k}\Delta_{w^{i_\bullet}_k\Lambda_j}(g)^{-a_{ji_k}}}{\Delta_{w^{i_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)\Delta_{w^{i_\bullet}_k\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)}
    \]
    for some non-zero constant $\lambda^{i_\bullet}_k\in k^\times$ that only depends on the reduced word $i_\bullet$ and the position $k$.
    \item If $(i_{s-1},i_s,i_{s+1})=(i,j,i)$ for $i$ and $j$ with $a_{ij}=a_{ji}=-1$, and we replace the product $y_{i_{s-1}}(a_{s-1})y_{i_s}(a_s)y_{i_{s+1}}(a_{s+1})$ in the definition of $y$ with $y_i(1)y_j(-a)y_i(-1)y_j(a)$, then (1) will still hold for all $k\neq s-1,s,s+1$ and 
    \[
    a=\lambda \dfrac{\prod_{r\neq i,j}\Delta_{w^{i_\bullet}_s\Lambda_r}(g)^{-a_{ri}-a_{rj}}}{\Delta_{w^{i_\bullet}_{s-2}\Lambda_i}(g)\Delta_{w^{i_\bullet}_{s+1}\Lambda_j}(g)}
    \]
    for some non-zero constant $\lambda\in k^\times$ that only depends on the reduced word $i_\bullet$ and the position $s$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
By following the proof of Proposition \ref{prop: rotation}, if we start with the configuration $(B_3,A^1,A_2)=(B, N^-, gN)$, then $((B^1)^t,(A_2)^t,(B^4)^t)=(B, N^-g^t, wB)$. We lift $B\xrightarrow{w}wB$ to the compatible pair $N\ext@arrow 0359\Rightarrowfill@{}{\overline{w}}\overline{w}N$, and divide the triangle spanned by $(A^1)^t$, $(A_2)^t$, and $(A^4)^t$ into smaller triangles as below. Each small triangle now has an angle unipotent $y_{i_k}(a_k)$ for $k=1,2,\dots, m$. 
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline=20]
    \node (1) at (0,0) [] {$(A^1)^t$};
    \node (2) at (1,2) [] {$(A_2)^t$};
    \node (4) at (2,0) [] {$(A^4)^t$};
    \draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (1) -- node [below] {$w$} (4);
    \draw [double, double distance=1pt](1) -- (2);
    \draw (2) -- (4);
\end{tikzpicture} =\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline=20]
\node (t) at (4,2) [] {$N^-g^t$};
\node (0) at (0,0) [] {$N$};
\node (1) at (2,0) [] {$\overline{s}_{i_1}N$};
\node (2) at (4,0) [] {$\overline{s}_{i_1}\overline{s}_{i_2}N$};
\node (3) at (6,0) [] {$\cdots$};
\node (4) at (8,0) [] {$\overline{w}N$};
\draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (0) -- node [below] {$s_{i_1}$} (1);
\draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (1) -- node [below] {$s_{i_2}$} (2);
\draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (2) --  (3);
\draw [-implies, double, double distance=1pt] (3) --  (4);
\draw [double, double distance=1pt] (t) -- (0);
\foreach \i in {1,2,4}
{
\draw (t) -- (\i);
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
By applying Lemma \ref{lem: Lusztig variables in terms of A variables} to each small triangle, we get that
\[
a_k=\frac{\prod_{j\neq i_k}\Delta_{\Lambda_j}\left(g^t\overline{w^{i_\bullet}_k}\right)^{-a_{ji_k}}}{\Delta_{\Lambda_{i_k}}\left(g^t\overline{w^{i_\bullet}_{k-1}}\right)\Delta_{\Lambda_{i_k}}\left(g^t\overline{w^{i_\bullet}_k}\right)}.
\]
Now (1) follows from the fact that for any $u\in W$ and fundamental weight $\Lambda_i$, 
\[
\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(g^t\overline{u})=\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(\overline{u}^tg)=\Delta_{\Lambda_i}(h_u\overline{u}^{-1}g)=\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, h_u\overline{u}^{-1}g\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\Lambda_i(h_u)\langle \xi_{u\Lambda_i},g\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\Lambda_i(h_u)\Delta_{u\Lambda_i}(g),
\]
where the maximal torus element $h_u\in T(k)$ exists by Lemma \ref{lem: transposition of Weyl group element lift}.

The formula for $a$ in claim (2) can be obtained in a similar way by replacing Lemma \ref{lem: Lusztig variables in terms of A variables} with Lemma \ref{lem: special case}.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop: y=y2y1} Suppose $w=w_1w_2$ with $l(w)=l(w_1)+l(w_2)$. Let $y_1\in N^-(w_1)$ and $y_2\in N^-(w_2)$. Then $y:=y_1y_2\in N^-(w)$. Let $g=\eta_{w}^{-1}(y)$. Then $g=g_2g_1$ with $g_1=\eta_{w_1}^{-1}(y_1)$ and $g_2\in N^-\cap \overline{w}_1N^-\overline{w}_1^{-1}$.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Let us consider the starting configuration in the left picture below. We apply the transposition to obtain the picture on the right.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline=20]
    \node (1) at (0,0) [] {$A_1$};
    \node (2) at (2,0) [] {$B_2$};
    \node (3) at (4,0) [] {$B_3$};
    \node (4) at (2,2) [] {$A^4$};
    \draw [double, double distance=1pt] (4) -- (1);
    \draw [->] (1) -- node [below] {$w_1$} (2);
    \draw                                          
    [->] (2) -- node [below] {$w_2$} (3);
    \draw (4) -- (2);
    \draw (4) -- (3);
    \draw [red, ->] (1.5,1.25) arc (-135:-90:0.5) node [below left] {$y_1$};
    \draw [red, <-] (2.5,1.25) arc (-45:-90:0.5) node [below right] {$y_2$};
\end{tikzpicture} \quad \rightsquigarrow \quad
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline=20]
    \node (1) at (4,2) [] {$(A_1)^t$};
    \node (2) at (2,2) [] {$(B_2)^t$};
    \node (3) at (0,2) [] {$(B_3)^t$};
    \node (4) at (2,0) [] {$(A^4)^t$};
    \draw [double, double distance=1pt] (4) -- (1);
    \draw [<-] (1) -- node [above] {$w_1^{-1}$} (2);
    \draw [<-] (2) -- node [above] {$w_2^{-1}$} (3);
    \draw (4) -- (2);
    \draw (4) -- (3);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
We then find the unique $B_5$ between $(B_3)^t$ and $(A^4)^t$ and the unique $B_6$ between $(B_2)^t$ and $B_5$ that satisfy the Tits (co)distance conditions below. Then $g=\eta_{w}^{-1}(y)=u(B_6, (A_1)^t, (B^4)^t)$.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline=20,scale=1.5]
    \node (1) at (4,2) [] {$(A_1)^t$};
    \node (2) at (2,2) [] {$(B_2)^t$};
    \node (3) at (0,2) [] {$(B_3)^t$};
    \node (4) at (2,0) [] {$(A^4)^t$};
    \node (5) at (0,0) [] {$B_5$};
    \node (6) at (1,1) [] {$B_6$};
    \draw [double, double distance=1pt] (4) -- (1);
    \draw [<-] (1) -- node [above] {$w_1^{-1}$} (2);
    \draw [<-] (2) -- node [above] {$w_2^{-1}$} (3);
    \draw (4) -- (2);
    \draw [->] (5) -- node [below] {$w_2$} (4);
    \draw (3) -- node [left] {$w_2^{-1}$} (5);
    \draw [->] (6) -- node [right] {$w_1$} (5);
    \draw (2) -- node [left] {$w_1^{-1}$} (6);
    \draw (1) -- (6);
    \draw [red, ->] (2.5,1.3) arc (-170:-120:0.5) node [above] {$g$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
On the other hand, we can break the edge $(B_2)^t\mbox{ --- } (A^4)^t$ in half to compute $g_1=\eta_{w_1}^{-1}(y_1)=u(B_7,(A_1)^t,(A^4)^t)$.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline=20,scale=2]
    \node (1) at (4,2) [] {$(A_1)^t$};
    \node (2) at (2,2) [] {$(B_2)^t$};
    \node (3) at (0,2) [] {$(B_3)^t$};
    \node (4) at (2,0) [] {$(A^4)^t$};
    \node (5) at (0,0) [] {$B_5$};
    \node (6) at (1,1) [] {$B_6$};
    \node (7) at (2,0.8) [] {$B_7$};
    \draw [double, double distance=1pt] (4) -- (1);
    \draw [<-] (1) -- node [above] {$w_1^{-1}$} (2);
    \draw [<-] (2) -- node [above] {$w_2^{-1}$} (3);
    \draw [->] (7) -- node [left] {$w_1$} (4);
    \draw (2) -- (7);
    \node at (2.2,1.7) [] {$w_1^{-1}$};
    \draw [->] (5) -- node [below] {$w_2$} (4);
    \draw (3) -- node [left] {$w_2^{-1}$} (5);
    \draw [->] (6) -- node [right] {$w_1$} (5);
    \draw (2) -- node [left] {$w_1^{-1}$} (6);
    \draw (1) -- (6);
    \draw (1) -- (7);
    \draw [red, ->] (2.4,0.95) arc (-140:-110:0.5) node [above] {$g_1$};
    \draw [red, ->] (2.2,1.3) arc (-170:-140:0.5) node [above] {$\quad g_2$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
By comparing the pictures we see that $g=g_2g_1$ with $g_2=u(B_6,(A_1)^t,B_7)\in N^-$. Now it remains to show that $g_2\in \overline{w}_1N^-\overline{w}_1^{-1}$. To see this, consider the following subgraph on the left.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.5, baseline=40]
    \node (7) at (0,0) [] {$B_6$};
    \node (6) at (2,0) [] {$B_7$};
    \node (2) at (1,1) [] {$(B_2)^t$};
    \node (3) at (1,2) [] {$(A_1)^t$};
    \draw (7) -- node [left] {$w_1^{-1}$} (2);
    \draw (6) -- node [right] {$w_1^{-1}$} (2);
    \draw [->] (2) -- node [right] {$w_1^{-1}$} (3);
    \draw (7) to [out=120,in=-180] (3); 
    \draw (6) to [out=60,in=0] (3);
\end{tikzpicture}\quad \sim \quad 
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.5, baseline=40]
    \node (7) at (0,0) [] {$B$};
    \node (6) at (2,0) [] {$g_2B$};
    \node (2) at (1,1) [] {$B^-w_1^{-1}$};
    \node (3) at (1,2) [] {$N^-$};
    \draw (7) -- node [left] {$w_1^{-1}$} (2);
    \draw (6) -- node [right] {$w_1^{-1}$} (2);
    \draw [->] (2) -- node [right] {$w_1^{-1}$} (3);
    \draw (7) to [out=120,in=-180] (3); 
    \draw (6) to [out=60,in=0] (3);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
Up to the action of a suitable element $G$, we can move the configuration so that $(A_1)^t$ becomes $N^-$ and $B_6$ becomes $B$. This forces $(B_2)^t$ to become $B^-w_1^{-1}$ and $B_7$ to become $g_2B$, as shown in the picture on the right. Since both $B_6\mbox{ --- } (A_1)^t$ and $B_7 \mbox{ --- } (A_1)^t$ share the same intermediate flag $(B_2)^t=B^-w_1^{-1}$, it follows that $g_2\cdot (B^-w_1^{-1})=B^-w_1^{-1}g_2^{-1}=B^-w_1^{-1}$, which is equivalent to $g_2\in w_1B^-w_1^{-1}$. But since $g_2$ is unipotent, it follows that $g_2\in N^-\cap \overline{w}_1N^-\overline{w}_1^{-1}$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{defn: A} Given $\imath\in I_\tau$ and a non-negative integer $n$, we define the scheme $\mathcal{A}^\imath(n)$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $\imath$ is standard, then $\mathcal{A}^\imath(n):=t^{nm_\imath}\mathcal{O}_\imath^\times$.
    \item If $\imath$ is exceptional with $m=2$, then depending on the parity of $n$, we have two subcases:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item if $n=2k$, then $\mathcal{A}^\imath(2k):=t^{k}\mathcal{O}^\times\times t^{2k}\mathcal{O}^\gamma$;
        \item if $n=2k+1$, then $\mathcal{A}^\imath(2k+1):=t^{(k+1)}\mathcal{O}\times t^{2k}(\mathcal{O}^\times)^\gamma$.
    \end{itemize}
    \item If $\imath$ is exceptional with $m=4$, then depending on the parity of $n$, we have two subcases:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item if $n=2k$, then $\mathcal{A}^\imath(2k):=t^{2k}\mathcal{O}_\imath\times t^{4k}(\mathcal{O}_\imath^\times)^\gamma$;
        \item if $n=2k+1$, then $\mathcal{A}^\imath(2k+1):=t^{2k}\mathcal{O}_\imath^\times \times t^{4(k+1)}\mathcal{O}_\imath^\gamma$.
    \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
For an $N$-tuple $n_\bullet=(n_1,\dots, n_N)$ of non-negative integers, we define 
\[
\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)=\prod_{k=1}^N \mathcal{A}^{\imath_k}(n_k).
\]
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{prop: A2n valuation} For the  $a$ defined in \eqref{eq: a} for an exceptional $\imath$, $\mathrm{val}(a)=nm_\imath$.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Let us first consider the case with $m=2$. Since $a=(b\gamma(b)+tc)/2$, $\mathrm{val}(b\gamma(b))$ is always even, and $\mathrm{val}(tc)$ is always odd, it follows that 
\[
\mathrm{val}(a)=\min\{2\mathrm{val}(b),\mathrm{val}(c)+1\}=\left\{\begin{array}{ll}2\mathrm{val}(b)=2k & \text{if $n=2k$,}\\
\mathrm{val}(c)+1=2k+1 & \text{if $n=2k+1$}.
\end{array}\right.
\]
For the case with $m=4$,  since $a=(-t^2b\gamma(b)+c)/2$, $\mathrm{val}(t^2b\gamma(b))\equiv 2 \mod 4$, and $\mathrm{val}(c)\equiv 0 \mod 4$, we get
\[
\mathrm{val}(a)=\min\{2\mathrm{val}(b)+2,\mathrm{val}(c)\}=\left\{\begin{array}{ll}\mathrm{val}(c)=4k & \text{if $n=2k$},\\
2\mathrm{val}(b)+2=4k+2 & \text{if $n=2k+1$}.\end{array}\right. \qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def: twist image} Let  $a_\bullet\in \mathcal{A}_{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$. We define
\[
y_{\imath_\bullet}=y_{\imath_1}y_{\imath_2}\cdots y_{\imath_N},
\]
where $y_{\imath_k}:=y_{\imath_k}(a_k)$ if $\imath_k$ is standard, and $y_{\imath_k}:=y_{\imath_k}(b_k,c_k)$ if $\imath_k$ is exceptional. By the fact that $y_i(f)\in N^-(s_i)(\mathcal{K})$ and $y_\imath(0,c)\in N^-(s_\imath)(\mathcal{K})$ for exceptional $\imath$ (Lemma \ref{lem: change of word in special case} (3)), we know that $y_{\imath_\bullet}\in N^-(w_0)(\mathcal{K})$. We define $g_{\imath_\bullet}:=\eta_{w_0}^{-1}(y_{\imath_\bullet})$. Since $y_{\imath_k}$ is $\sigma$-invariant for each $k$ (Proposition \ref{prop: sigma invariant unipotent}), by Lemma \ref{lem: sigma invariant}, we know that $g_{\imath_\bullet}$ is also $\sigma$-invariant and hence $[g_{\imath_\bullet}]$ is an element of $\mathscr{G}\!r$. We denote the resulting map by 
\[
\Phi:\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)\rightarrow \mathscr{G}\!r.
\]
Lemma \ref{lem: limit} implies that, despite there being two ways of defining $y_\imath$ for an exceptional $\imath$ depending on whether $b$ vanishes or not, the resulting map $\Phi$ is still algebraic.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem: ratio}  Let $\imath_\bullet=(\imath_1, \dots, \imath_N)$ be a reduced word of $w_0\in W^\tau$, and set $w^{\imath_\bullet}_k:=s_{\imath_1}\cdots s_{\imath_k}$.  Let $y_{\imath_\bullet}$ and $g_{\imath_\bullet}$ be as in Definition \ref{def: twist image}. For any $\Lambda_i$ and $v\in V(\Lambda_i)$, we  have, for $1\leq k\leq N$,
\[
\mathrm{val}(\langle \xi_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_k\Lambda_i}, g_{\imath_\bullet}\cdot v\rangle)\geq \mathrm{val}(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_k\Lambda_i}(g_{\imath_\bullet})).
\]
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Let $w_1:=w^{\imath_\bullet}_k$ and $w_2:=w_1^{-1}w$. Let $y_1:=y_{\imath_1}\cdots y_{\imath_k}$ and let $y_2:=y_1^{-1}y$. Then by Proposition \ref{prop: y=y2y1}, we can write $g=g_2g_1$ with $g_1=\eta_{w_1}^{-1}(y_1)$ and $g_2\in N^-\cap \overline{w}_1N^-\overline{w}_1^{-1}$. On the one hand, since $y_1=\eta_{w_1}(g_1)$, it follows that $y_1b=g_1^t\overline{w}_1$ for some $b\in B$, which is equivalent to $g_1=\overline{w}_1lty_1^t$ for some $l\in N^-$ and $t\in T$. On the other hand, $g_2\in N^-\cap \overline{w}_1N^-\overline{w}_1^{-1}$ implies that $g_2^{-1}\cdot \xi_{w_1\Lambda_i}=\xi_{w_1\Lambda_i}$. By combining these two facts, we get
\[
g^{-1}\cdot \xi_{w_1\Lambda_i}=g_1^{-1}g_2^{-1}\cdot \xi_{w_1\Lambda_i}=g_1^{-1}\cdot \xi_{w_1\Lambda_i}=(y_1^t)^{-1}t^{-1}l^{-1}\overline{w}_1^{-1}\xi_{w_1\Lambda_i}=\Lambda_i(t)(y_1^t)^{-1}\cdot \xi_{\Lambda_i}.
\]
Thus, we have
\[
\Delta_{w_1\Lambda_i}(g)=\langle \xi_{w_1\Lambda_i},g\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\langle g^{-1}\cdot \xi_{w_1\Lambda_i},v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\Lambda_i(t)\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, y_1^t\cdot v_{\Lambda_i}\rangle=\Lambda_i(t),
\]
and
\[
\langle\xi_{w_1\Lambda_i}, g\cdot v\rangle=\langle g^{-1}\cdot \xi_{w_1\Lambda_i}, v\rangle=\Lambda_i(t)\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, y_1^t\cdot v\rangle=\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, y_1^t\cdot v\rangle\Delta_{w_1\Lambda_i}(g).
\]
It remains to argue that $\mathrm{val}(\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, y_1^t\cdot v\rangle)\geq 0$. Let $s$ be an index with $1\leq s\leq k$. When $\imath_s$ is standard, $\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, y_{\imath_s}^t\cdot v\rangle$ is a polynomial (over $\mathcal{O}$) in $a_s$, which has a non-negative valuation. When $\imath_s$ is exceptional,  $\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, y_{\imath_s}^t\cdot v\rangle$ is a polynomial (over $\mathcal{O}$) in $a_s$, $b_s$, and $\gamma(b_s)$ if $b_s\neq 0$, and is a polynomial (over $\mathcal{O}$) in $a_s$ if $b_s=0$ (c.f. Proof of Lemma \ref{lem: limit}); in either case, $\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, y_{\imath_s}^t\cdot v\rangle$ has a non-negative valuation as well. Thus, we can conclude that $\mathrm{val}(\langle \xi_{\Lambda_i}, y_1^t\cdot v\rangle)\geq 0$ and hence the lemma is true.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
The image of the map $\Phi: \mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)\rightarrow \mathscr{G}\!r$ defined in Definition \ref{def: twist image} lies in $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
For convenience, we write $x\underset{\rm val}{\sim} y$ if $x, y \in \mathcal{K}^\times$ and $\mathrm{val}(x)=\mathrm{val}(y)$.
Note that multiplying by a nonzero element in $k$, or changing a factor under the action of a power of $\gamma$, does not change the valuation. 

                                                                                                                                                                           

Let $g:= g_{\imath_\bullet}$. For each $\jmath\in I_\tau$, let $\Lambda_j$ be a lift of $\Lambda_\jmath$; since $g\in N^-(w_0)(\mathcal{K})^\sigma\subset N^-(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$, we have
\begin{align*}
D_{\Lambda_\jmath}([g])=&\min_{v\in \overline{V}_{\Lambda_\jmath}}\mathrm{val}(\langle \xi_{\Lambda_j},g\cdot v\rangle)=\min_{v\in \overline{V}_{\Lambda_\jmath}} \mathrm{val}(\langle g^{-1}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda_j}, v\rangle )\\
=&\min_{v\in \overline{V}_{\Lambda_\jmath}}\mathrm{val}(\langle  \xi_{\Lambda_j},v\rangle)=\mathrm{val}(\langle  \xi_{\Lambda_j},v_{\Lambda_j}\rangle)=0=\mathrm{val}(\Delta_{\Lambda_j}(g)).
\end{align*}



For the rest of the chamber weights of the form $w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{\imath_k}$ with standard $\imath_k$, Lemma \ref{lem: minor under gamma action} and Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula} imply that, for the chosen representative $i_k$ of $\imath_k$, 
\[
a_k=\lambda^{\imath_\bullet}_k\frac{\prod_{j\neq i_k}\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_j}(g)^{-a_{ji_k}}}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)}\underset{\rm val}{\sim} \frac{\prod_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_j}(g)^{-a_{\jmath\imath_k}}}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)},
\]
where the $j$'s in the last expression are the chosen representatives of $\jmath$.

For the chamber weights of the form $w^{\imath_\bullet}_{ k}\Lambda_{\imath_k}$ with exceptional $\imath_k=\{l,l+1\}$ in the case of $A_{2l}^{(2)}$, let us consider the following.
\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tikzpicture}
        \foreach \i in {1,2,3}
        {
        \draw [gray] (0,\i) -- (6,\i);
        }
        \draw [gray](1.5,1) -- node [black] {$s_l$} (1.5,2);
        \draw [gray](3,2) -- node [black] {$s_{l+1}$} (3,3);
        \draw [gray](4.5,1) -- node [black] {$s_l$} (4.5,2);
        \node at (0,1.5) [] {$\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}$};
        \node at (3,1.5) [] {$\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_i\Lambda_l}$};
        \node at (6,1.5) [] {$\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_l}$};
        \node at (1,2.5) [] {$\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{l+1}}$};
        \node at (5,2.5) [] {$\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}$};
        \node at (3,0.5) [] {$\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l-1}}$};
        \node at (3,3.5) [] {$\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+2}}$};
    \end{tikzpicture}
    \caption{Local picture of generalized minors near an exceptional $\imath_k$ in $A_{2l}^{(2)}$.}
\end{figure}
If $b_k\neq 0$, by Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula} (1) and Eq. \eqref{eq: root subgroup element for A2n}, we see that there are non-zero constants $\lambda,\lambda'\in k^\times$ such that,
\[
\lambda\frac{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+2}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)}=\left\{\begin{array}{ll}
    b_k & \text{if $m=2$},  \\
    tb_k & \text{if $m=4$},
\end{array}\right.
\]
and
\[
\lambda'\frac{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l-1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_l}(g)}=\left\{\begin{array}{ll} a_k/b_k & \text{if $m=2$},\\
a_k/(tb_k) & \text{if $m=4$}. \end{array}\right.
\]
Thus, regardless of whether $m=2$ or $m=4$, we have
\[
a_k=\lambda\lambda'\frac{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l-1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+2}}(g)}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l}}(g)}\underset{\rm val}{\sim}\frac{\prod_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_j}(g)^{-a_{\jmath\imath_k}}}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l}}(g)}.
\]

If $b_k=0$, by Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula} (2) and Eq. \eqref{eq: root subgroup element for A2n}, we can also conclude that for some non-zero constant $\lambda\in k^\times$,
\[
a_k=\lambda\frac{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l-1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+2}}(g)}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)}\underset{\rm val}{\sim}\frac{\prod_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_j}(g)^{-a_{\jmath\imath_k}}}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l}}(g)}.
\]

These relations between $a_k$'s and the generalized minors of $g$ allow us to compute $\mathrm{val}(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g))$ recursively as:
\begin{align*}
\mathrm{val}\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)\right)=&-\left(\sum_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}a_{\jmath\imath_k}\mathrm{val}\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_j}(g)\right)\right)-\mathrm{val}\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)\right)-\mathrm{val}\left(a_k\right)\\
=&-\left(\sum_{\jmath\neq \imath_k}a_{\jmath\imath_k}\mathrm{val}\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_j}(g)\right)\right)-\mathrm{val}\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)\right)-n_km_{\imath_k}.
\end{align*}
By comparison with Proposition \ref{prop: A(n)}, we see that it suffices to prove $D_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k} \Lambda_{\imath_k}}([g])=\mathrm{val}\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g)\right)$ for all $1\leq k\leq N$. By definition of $D_\gamma$ (Equation \eqref{D_def}), we see that on the one hand,
\[
D_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{\imath_k}}([g])= \min_{v\in \overline{V}(\Lambda_{\imath_k})}\mathrm{val}\left(\left\langle \xi_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{i_k}},g\cdot v\right\rangle\right)\leq \mathrm{val}\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k} \Lambda_{i_k}}(g)\right).
\]
On the other hand, Lemma \ref{lem: ratio} gives the reverse inequality. Thus, we can conclude that 
\[
D_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k} \Lambda_{\imath_k}}([g])=\mathrm{val}(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{ k}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g))
\]
for all $1\leq k\leq N$, which shows that the image $[g]$ lands inside $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{prop: surjective} The map $\Phi:\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)\rightarrow A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ in Definition \ref{def: twist image} is surjective.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Suppose $L=[g]$ for some $g\in N^-(\mathcal{K})^\sigma$. By Lemma \ref{lem: representative} we may assume without loss of generality that $D_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_k\Lambda_{\imath_k}}([g])=\mathrm{val}(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{i_k}}(g))$ for all $1\leq k\leq N$.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            
Note that except for the generalized minor $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)$ when $\imath_k=\{l,l+1\}$ is exceptional, all other generalized minor of $g$ appear in Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula} are $\underset{\rm val}{\sim}\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_s\Lambda_{i_s}}(g)$ for some $s$, and hence are non-zero because they have finite valuations. In particular, $\Delta_{w_0\Lambda_i}(g)\neq 0$ for all $\Lambda_i$ and therefore $g\in N^-(w_0)(\mathcal{K})$ (Lemma \ref{lem: in N^-(w_0)}). 

Let $y:=\eta_{w_0}(g)$. We claim that $y$ admits a factorization $y=y_{\imath_1}y_{\imath_2}\cdots y_{\imath_N}$, where each $y_{\imath_k}$ carries a parameter $a_k$ if $\imath_k$ is standard, and two parameters $b_k$ and $c_k$ if $\imath_k$ is exceptional.
