To see this, note that by Lemma \ref{lem: relative position} (1), there exists intermediate $B_1,B_2,\dots, B_{N-1}$ that fit compatibly in between $B\xrightarrow{w_0}yB$ as 
\[
B=B_0\xrightarrow{s_{\imath_1}}B_1\xrightarrow{s_{\imath_2}}B_2\xrightarrow{s_{\imath_3}} \cdots \xrightarrow{s_{\imath_{N-1}}} B_{N-1}\xrightarrow{s_{\imath_N}}yB=B_N,
\]
and even more decorated flags that sit within each $B_{k-1}\xrightarrow{s_{\imath_k}}B_k$ depending on the factorization of $s_{\imath_k}$ (Eq. \eqref{eq: s_i}). Moreover, up to a diagonal $G$-action, we can move (and lift) these flags into a compatible configuration of decorated flags as in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula}. Now the non-vanishing of $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_s\Lambda_{i_s}}(g)$ for all $1\leq s\leq N$ implies that $N^-\mbox{ --- }B_k$ for every $k$, and therefore the angle unipotents $u(B_{k-1},N^-,B_k)$ are well-defined. Moreover, if $\imath_k$ is standard, the angle unipotent $u(B_{k-1},N^-,B_k)$ must be of the form $y_{\imath_k}(a_k)$ for some $a_k$ (Lemmas \ref{lem: minor under gamma action} and \ref{lem: Lusztig variables in terms of A variables}); if $\imath_k$ is exceptional, the angle unipotent $u(B_{k-1},N^-B_k)$ must be of the form $y_{\imath_k}(b_k,c_k)$ for some $b_k$ and $c_k$ (Lemmas \ref{lem: minor under gamma action} and \ref{lem: Lusztig variables in terms of A variables} when $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)\neq 0$ and Proposition \ref{prop: vanishing minor implies special angle unipotent} and Lemma \ref{lem: special case} when $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)= 0$). This proves the claim of the factorization $y=y_{\imath_1}y_{\imath_2}\cdots y_{\imath_N}$.

Next, we need to argue that the parameters $a_k$, $b_k$, and $c_k$ are from the correct $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_k}(n_k)$. Note that if $\imath_k$ is standard, we know that $a_k\in \mathcal{K}_{\imath_k}$ by Corollary \ref{prop: quotient in K}, and Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula} dictates that $\mathrm{val}(a_k)=n_km_{\imath_k}$, and therefore $a_k\in t^{n_km_{\imath_k}}\mathcal{O}_{\imath_k}^\times$.

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 

It remains to consider the case of $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with exceptional $\imath_k=\{l,l+1\}$.                                                                                                                  
Let us first consider the case with $m=2$, for which $m_{\imath_k}=1$ and hence $\mathcal{K}_{\imath_k}=\mathcal{K}$.                                                    
On the one hand, if $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)\neq 0$, then both 
\[
b_k=\lambda\dfrac{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+2}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)} \quad \text{and} \quad
\frac{a_k}{b_k}=\lambda'\frac{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l-1}}(g)}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_k\Lambda_l}(g)} 
\]
are non-zero, and hence we can compute $a_k$ by taking their product and then compute $c_k$ from Eq. \eqref{eq: a}. In particular, since $c_k=(2a_k-b_k\gamma(b_k))/t=(a_k-\gamma(a_k))/t$, $c_k$ must be an element of $\mathcal{K}^\gamma$. 

On the other hand, if $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)=0$, then $\gamma\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)\right)=0$. It then follows from Propositions \ref{prop: vanishing minor implies special angle unipotent} and \ref{prop: minor recursive formula} (2) that the angle unipotent element $y_{\imath_k}=y_l(1)y_{l+1}(-a_k)y_l(-1)y_{l+1}(a_k)$ for
\[
a_k=\lambda\frac{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l-1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+2}}(g)}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)}.
\]
Note that the numerator is $\gamma$-invariant since $l+2=\tau(l-1)$. For the denominator, by Eq. \eqref{eq: minor identity}, we can deduce that 
\[
\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)=-\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_l}(g)
\]
But then by Lemmas \ref{lem: minor under gamma action} and \ref{lem: pairing mod 4} (1), we know that
\begin{align*}
\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_l}(g)=&(-1)^{\langle h, w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l-\Lambda_l+w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}-\Lambda_{l+1}\rangle}\gamma\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\right)\\
=&\gamma\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\right).
\end{align*}
Thus, the denominator of $a_k$ is $\gamma$-anti-invariant and therefore $\gamma(a_k)=-a_k$. Comparing this with Eq. \eqref{eq: a}, we see that $b_k=0$ and $c_k=2a_k/t\in \mathcal{K}^\gamma$.

In either subcase of the $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with $m=2$ case, we have $\mathrm{val}(a_k)=n_k$ by Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula}. Since both $2\mathrm{val}(b_k)$ and $\mathrm{val}(c_k)$ are even numbers, $n_k=\mathrm{val}(a_k)=\min\{2\mathrm{val}(b_k),\mathrm{val}(c_k)+1\}$, which equals $2\mathrm{val}(b_k)$ if $n_k$ is even, and $\mathrm{val}(c_k)+1$ if $n_k$ is odd, yielding the correct $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_k}(n_k)$.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               

For the case of $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with $m=4$, we have $m_{\imath_k}=2$ and hence $\mathcal{K}_{\imath_k}=k(\!(t^2)\!)$. If $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)\neq 0$, then we can compute $a_k, b_k, c_k$ similar to the $m=2$ case above; in particular, by Corollaries \ref{cor: A2l with m=4} and \ref{cor: A2n m=4 middle minor}, we know that $a_k,b_k\in \mathcal{K}_{\imath_k}$, and hence $c_k=2a_k+t^2b_k\gamma(b_k)=a_k+\gamma(a_k)\in \mathcal{K}_{\imath_k}^\gamma$.

If $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}s_l\Lambda_l}(g)=0$, then we again set $a_k=\lambda\dfrac{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l-1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+2}}(g)}{\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)}$ as in Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula} (2). Note that the numerator is again $\gamma$-invariant, and by Eq. \eqref{eq: minor identity}, we again have $\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)=-\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_l}(g)$.
But this time, Lemmas \ref{lem: minor under gamma action} and \ref{lem: pairing mod 4} (2) implies that
\begin{align*}
\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_l}(g)=&\epsilon^{\langle h, w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l-\Lambda_l+w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}-\Lambda_{l+1}\rangle}\gamma\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\right)\\
=&-\gamma\left(\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k-1}\Lambda_l}(g)\Delta_{w^{\imath_\bullet}_{k}\Lambda_{l+1}}(g)\right).
\end{align*}
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  
Thus, we can conclude that the denominator of $a_k$, and hence $a_k$ itself, is $\gamma$-invariant. Comparing this with Eq. \eqref{eq: a}, we see that $b_k=0$ again and $c_k=2a_k\in \mathcal{K}_{\imath_k}^\gamma$.

In either subcase of the $A_{2l}^{(2)}$ with $m=4$ case, we have $\mathrm{val}(a_k)=2n_k$ by Proposition \ref{prop: minor recursive formula}. Since both $2\mathrm{val}(b_k)$ and $\mathrm{val}(c_k)$ are multiples of $4$, $2n_k=\mathrm{val}(a_k)=\min\{2\mathrm{val}(b_k)+2,\mathrm{val}(c_k)\}$, which equals $\mathrm{val}(c_k)$ if $n_k$ is even, and $2\mathrm{val}(b_k)+2$ if $n_k$ is odd, yielding the correct $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_k}(n_k)$ again.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor:irred}
$A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ is irreducible.
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Since the image of an irreducible space is irreducible, the corollary follows from the fact that $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ is irreducible and the map $\Phi:\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)\rightarrow A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ is surjective (Theorem \ref{prop: surjective}).
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm_MV_constr}
In the decomposition (\ref{eq:ST-decomp}), for each $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ 
the closure $\overline{A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)}$ is an irreducible component of  $\overline{S_\mu\cap T_0}$ and each component of $\overline{S_\mu\cap T_0}$ arises exactly once this way. In particular, the variety $\overline{A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)}$ has dimension $\langle \mu, \rho \rangle$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Let $k(\mu)$ be the Kostant partition function for the Satake group $(\check{G})^\tau$.  

 
By Proposition \ref{T_S_prop}, 
we can choose a sufficient dominant $\eta\in X_*(H)^+_\tau$, such that
\[ \overline{ S_{\mu+\eta} \cap T_{ \eta}  }= \overline{ \mathscr{G}\!r_{\mu+\eta} \cap T_{\eta } }.\]
By Theorem \ref{thm_ramified_Satake}, the number of irreducible components of $\overline{ \mathscr{G}\!r_{\mu+\eta} \cap T_{\eta } }$ equals $\dim V_{\eta+\mu}(\eta)$. It is well-known that when $\eta$ is sufficiently dominant, $\dim V_{\eta+\mu}(\eta)=k(\mu)$. 
Note that, $\overline{S_\mu\cap T_0}$ is a translation of $ \overline{ S_{\mu+\eta} \cap T_{ \eta}  }$. 
Thus, $\overline{S_\mu\cap T_0}$ has $k(\mu)$ many irreducible components. 

By \eqref{eq:ST-decomp}, $S_\mu\cap  T_0$ is a disjoint union of $k(\mu)$ many strata $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$. 
By Corollary \ref{cor:irred}, $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ is irreducible.
  By \cite[Lemma 4.3]{Kam},  all $\overline{A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)}$ are distinct irreducible components of   $\overline{S_\mu\cap T_0}$ of dimension  $\langle \mu, \rho \rangle$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop: A1xA1} If $y_\imath(a'_1)y_\jmath(a'_2)=y_\jmath(a_1)y_\imath(a_2)$, then
    \[
    a'_1=a_2 \quad \text{and} \quad a'_2=a_1,
    \]
    and therefore
    \[
    n'_1=n_2 \quad \text{and} \quad n'_2=n_1.
    \]
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{prop: A2} If $y_\imath(a'_1)y_\jmath(a'_2)y_\imath(a'_3)=y_\jmath(a_1)y_\imath(a_2)y_\jmath(a_3)$, then there is a non-empty open subset in $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$, over which
    \[
    n'_1=n_2+n_3-\min\{n_1,n_3\}, \quad n'_2=\min\{n_1,n_3\}, \quad n'_3=n_1+n_2-\min\{n_1,n_3\}.
    \]
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
If $|\imath|=|\jmath|=1$, then by Eq. \eqref{eq: Lusztig transform}
    \[
    y_j(a_1)y_i(a_2)y_j(a_3)=y_i\left(\frac{a_2a_3}{a_1+a_3}\right)y_j(a_1+a_3)y_i\left(\frac{a_1a_2}{a_1+a_3}\right),
    \]
    we see that, over the open subset where the leading coefficients of $a_1$ and $a_3$ do not cancel, we have the desired identity.
    
    Suppose $|\imath|=|\jmath|=2$. Let us fix $i\in \imath$ and $j\in \jmath$ such that $a_{ij}=-1$. Let $i'=\tau(i)$ and $j'=\tau(j)$. Then it is necessary that $a_{ii'}=a_{jj'}=a_{ij'}=a_{i'j}=0$. This implies that
    \begin{align*}
    y_\jmath(a_1)y_\imath(a_2)y_\jmath(a_3)=& y_j(a_1)y_{j'}(\epsilon^{-\alpha_j(h)}\gamma(a_1))y_i(a_2)y_{i'}(\epsilon^{-\alpha_i(h)}\gamma(a_2))y_j(a_3)y_{j'}(\epsilon^{-\alpha_j(h)}\gamma(a_3))\\
    =& y_j(a_1)y_i(a_2)y_j(a_3)y_{j'}(\epsilon^{-\alpha_j(h)}\gamma(a_1))y_{i'}(\epsilon^{-\alpha_i(h)}\gamma(a_2))y_{j'}(\epsilon^{-\alpha_j(h)}\gamma(a_3)).
    \end{align*}
    A similar identity can also be derived for $y_\imath(a'_1)y_\jmath(a'_2)y_\imath(a'_3)$. By applying Eq. \eqref{eq: Lusztig transform}, we can deduce the same desired identity over the open subset where the leading coefficients of $a_1$ and $a_3$ do not cancel each other.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def: polynomial}                                                                                                                                                                                                    
    Let $\gamma$ be the field automorphism  of $\mathcal{K}={\mathrm{k}}(\!(t)\!)$ that fixes ${\mathrm{k}}$ pointwise and sends $t\mapsto \epsilon^{-1}t$, where $\epsilon$ is a primitive $m$th root of unity. Let $F$ be a polynomial over ${\mathrm{k}}$ in the variables $\gamma^j(x_i)$ with  $1\leq i \leq n$ and $0\leq j < m$. 
    When computing the degree vectors of monomials,  we identify the variables $x_i, \gamma(x_i), \ldots, \gamma^{m-1}(x_i)$ for each $i$. The \emph{Newton polytope} of $F$ is  the convex hull of the degree vectors of the monomials appearing in $F$. A degree vector is  \emph{extremal} if it is a vertex of the Newton polytope of $F$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{exmp: polynomial} Suppose $m=3$. Let  $\dot{x}:=\gamma(x)$ and $\ddot{x}:=\gamma^2(x)$. Set $N(x):=x\dot{x}\ddot{x}$ and $C(x):=x+\dot{x}+\ddot{x}$. Consider the polynomial \begin{align*}F&=N(x_1+\dot{x}_3)x_2+C(x_2^3\dot{x}_3)\\
&=N(x_1)x_2+C(x_1\dot{x}_1x_3)x_2+C(\dot{x}_1x_3\dot{x}_3)x_2+x_2N(x_3)+C(x_2^3\dot{x}_3)
    \end{align*}The degree vectors of the monomials appearing in the expansion of $F$ are 
    \[
(3,1,0),\quad (2,1,1),\quad (1,1,2),\quad (0,1,3),\quad (0,3,1).
    \]
    Each of $(3,1,0)$ and $(0,1,3)$ occurs for a single monomial, whereas each of $(2,1,1)$, $(1,1,2)$, and $(0,3,1)$ occurs for three monomials.
 The extremal degree vectors of $F$ are $(3,1,0)$, $(0,1,3)$, and $(0,3,1)$. Hence, the Newton polytope of $F$ is a triangle.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem: non-zero coefficient} Let $F$ be a polynomial as in Definition \ref{def: polynomial}. If each extremal degree vector $d$ of $F$ corresponds to one and only one term in $F$, then for generic $f_1,\dots, f_n\in \mathcal{K}$,
    \[
    \mathrm{val}(F(f_1,\dots, f_n))=\min_{\text{extremal $d$}}\{d\cdot \nu\},
    \]
    where $\nu:=(\mathrm{val}(f_1),\dots, \mathrm{val}(f_n))$.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Suppose for each index $i$, $f_i=u_it^{\nu_i}+\text{higher terms in $t$}$ with $u_i\neq 0$. Then $\gamma^n(f_i)=\epsilon^{-n\nu_i} u_it^{\nu_i}+\text{higher terms in $t$}$.                                                                               
                                                                                                                                                 
    Let $T$ be a monomial appearing in $F$ with coefficient $c_T\neq 0\in {\mathrm{k}}$ and degree vector $d$.                        
    After plugging all the $f_i$'s, we get
    \[
    T(f_1,\dots, f_n)=c_T\epsilon^{n_T}u^dt^{d\cdot \nu}+\text{higher terms in $t$},
    \]
    where $n_T$ is an integer determined by which $\gamma$-powered variables occur in $T$ and their respective exponents, $u^d:=\prod_i u_i^{d_i}$, and $\nu=(\nu_1,\dots, \nu_n)$. 
    
    Now suppose that each extremal degree vector $d$ of $F$ corresponds to exactly one term $T_d$ in $F$. For any given tuple of valuations $\nu$, define $\omega:=\min_d\{d\cdot \nu\}$ where the minimum is taken over all degree vectors of $F$. Then the terms in $F(f_1,\dots, f_n)$ that possibly achieve this minimum valuation are of the form
    \[
    \left(\sum_{\substack{\text{extremal $d$} \\ d\cdot \nu=\omega}}c_{T_d}\epsilon^{n_{T_d}}u^d+\sum_{\substack{\text{non-extremal $d$} \\ d\cdot\nu=\omega}}
    \cdots \right)t^\omega.
    \]
    In particular, the first sum over extremal degrees $d$ is non-empty. Thus, for any given tuple of valuations $\nu$, $\sum_{\substack{\text{extremal $d$} \\ d\cdot \nu=\omega}}c_{T_d}\epsilon^{n_{T_d}}u^d+\sum_{\substack{\text{non-extremal $d$} \\ d\cdot\nu=\omega}}
    \cdots$ is a non-zero polynomial, and hence we can always find a generic $u_i$ for each $f_i$ such that $\mathrm{val}(F(f_1,\dots, f_n))=\omega$.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proposition-15: body_md -->
\label{prop: B2} If $y_\imath(a'_1)y_\jmath(a'_2)y_\imath(a'_3)y_\jmath(a'_4)=y_\jmath(a_1)y_\imath(a_2)y_\jmath(a_3)y_\imath(a_4)$, then there is a non-empty open subset in $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$, over which
    \[
    n'_1=n_2+2n_3+n_4-p, \quad n'_2=p-q, \quad n'_3=2q-p, \quad n'_4=n_1+n_2+n_3-q
    \]
    where
    \[
    p:=\min\{2n_1+n_2,2n_1+n_4,2n_3+n_4\} \quad \text{and} \quad q:=\min\{n_1+n_2,n_1+n_4,n_3+n_4\}.
    \]
<!-- end proposition-15 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
There are two possibilities: either $\imath=\{i,i'\}$ and $\jmath=\{j\}$ with $a_{ij}=a_{i'j}=-1$ in types $A_{2l-1}^{(2)}$, $D_{l+1}^{(2)}$, or $E_6^{(2)}$, or $\imath=\{l,l+1\}$ and $\jmath=\{l-1,l+2\}$ in type $A_{2l}^{(2)}$. 

    In the first case where $\imath=\{i,i'\}$ and $\jmath=\{j\}$ with $a_{ij}=a_{i'j}=-1$ in types $A_{2l-1}^{(2)}$, $D_{l+1}^{(2)}$, or $E_6^{(2)}$, it is necessary that $m=2$ and $\epsilon=-1$. For notation convenience, let us define 
    for $f\in \mathcal{K}$,
    \[
    \overline{f}:=\epsilon^{-\alpha_i(h)}\gamma(f) \quad \text{and} \quad N(f):=f\overline{f}\in \mathcal{K}^\gamma.
    \]
    Note that by construction, 
    \[
    y_\jmath(a_1)y_\imath(a_2)y_\jmath(a_3)y_\imath(a_4)=y_j(a_1)y_i(a_2)y_{i'}(\epsilon^{-\alpha_i(h)}\gamma(a_2))y_j(a_3)y_i(a_4)y_{i'}(\epsilon^{-\alpha_i(h)}\gamma(a_4)),
    \]
    where $a_1,a_3\in \mathcal{K}^\gamma={\mathrm{k}}(\!(t^2)\!)$. In order to get to $y_\imath(a'_1)y_\jmath(a'_2)y_\imath(a'_3)y_\jmath(a'_4)$, we can apply the following braid moves in $W$:
    \begin{align*}
    &s_js_is_{i'}s_js_is_{i'}=s_js_{i'}s_is_js_is_{i'}=s_js_{i'}s_js_is_js_{i'}=s_{i'}s_js_{i'}s_is_js_{i'}\\=&s_{i'}s_js_is_{i'}s_js_{i'}=s_{i'}s_js_is_js_{i'}s_j=s_{i'}s_is_js_is_{i'}s_j=s_is_{i'}s_js_is_{i'}s_j.
    \end{align*}
    By applying Eq. \ref{eq: Lusztig transform} repetitively, we get that
    \[
    a'_1=\frac{a_2a_3\overline{a}_4}{\overline{P}}, \quad a'_2=\frac{N(P)}{Q}, \quad a'_3=\frac{Q}{\overline{P}}, \quad a'_4=\frac{a_1a_3N(a_2)}{Q},
    \]
    where
    \[        P=a_1(a_2+a_4)+a_3a_4 \quad \text{and} \quad        Q=a_1N(a_2+a_4)+a_3N(a_4).
    \]
    In particular, $Q\in \mathcal{K}^\gamma$. Note that $\mathrm{val}(a_1)=2n_1$, $\mathrm{val}(a_2)=n_2$, $\mathrm{val}(a_3)=2n_3$, and $\mathrm{val}(a_4)=n_4$; over the open subset where the leading coefficients of the terms of $P$ and $Q$ do not cancel, we have
    \[
    \mathrm{val}(P)=\min\{2n_1+n_2,2n_1+n_4,2n_3+n_4\}=p,
    \]
    \[
    \mathrm{val}(Q)=2\min\{n_1+n_2,n_1+n_4,n_3+n_4\}=2q.
    \]
    Thus, we can conclude that
    \[
    n'_1=\mathrm{val}(a'_1)=n_2+2n_3+n_4-p, \quad \quad n'_2=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(a'_2)=p-q,
    \]
    \[
    n'_3=\mathrm{val}(a'_3)=2q-p, \quad \quad n'_4=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(a'_4)=n_1+n_2+n_3-q.
    \]


                                                                                 

    In the second case where $\imath=\{l,l+1\}$ and $\jmath=\{l-1,l+2\}$ in type $A_{2l}^{(2)}$, the element $y_\imath$ is defined by Eq. \eqref{eq: root subgroup element for A2n}, and $\alpha_{l-1}(h)=\alpha_{l+2}(h)=0$. Note that regardless of whether $m=2$ or $4$, the actions of $\gamma$ on the $a_i$'s and $b_i$'s are all of order $2$; we therefore adopt the shorthand notation $\overline{x}:=\gamma(x)$ and define $N(x):=x\overline{x}$ for all $x\in \mathcal{K}^{\gamma^2}$; also, for notation simplicity, let us set $l=2$.                                                                                                
    Now, the product $y_\jmath(a_1)y_\imath(b_2,c_2)y_\jmath(a_3)y_\imath(b_4,c_4)$ is equal to
    \[
    \left\{\begin{array}{ll}
       y_1(a_1)y_4(\overline{a}_1)y_2\left(\frac{\overline{a}_2}{b_2}\right)y_3(b_2)y_2\left(\frac{a_2}{b_2}\right)y_1(a_3)y_4(\overline{a}_3)y_2\left(\frac{\overline{a}_4}{b_4}\right)y_3(b_4)y_2\left(\frac{a_4}{b_4}\right)  & \text{if $m=2$}, \\
       y_1(a_1)y_4(\overline{a}_1)y_2\left(\frac{-\overline{a}_2}{tb_2}\right)y_3(tb_2)y_2\left(\frac{a_2}{tb_2}\right)y_1(a_3)y_4(\overline{a}_3)y_2\left(\frac{-\overline{a}_4}{tb_4}\right)y_3(tb_4)y_2\left(\frac{a_4}{tb_4}\right) & \text{if $m=4$}.
    \end{array}\right.
    \]
    In order to get to $y_\imath(b'_1,c'_1)y_\jmath(a'_2)y_\imath(b'
    _3,c'_3)y_\jmath(a'_4)$, we can apply the following braid moves in $W$:
    \begin{align*}
        &{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}\\
        =&{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}\\
        =&{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}={\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}={\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}\\
        =&{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{red}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}.
        \end{align*}
    By applying Eq. \eqref{eq: Lusztig transform} repetitively, we get that when $m=2$:
    \[
    a'_1=\frac{a_2N(a_3)a_4}{\overline{P}}, \quad b'_1=\frac{(a_1\overline{a}_4b_2+a_1a_2b_4+a_3\overline{a}_4b_2)\overline{a}_3}{P}, \quad a'_2=\frac{\overline{P}}{\overline{Q}},
    \]
    \[
    a'_3=\frac{N(Q)}{P}, \quad b'_3=\frac{(\overline{a}_1b_2+\overline{a}_1b_4+\overline{a}_3b_4)Q}{P}, \quad a'_4=\frac{\overline{a}_1a_2a_3}{\overline{Q}},
    \]
    where
    \[
    P=N(a_1)\overline{a}_2+N(a_1+a_3)\overline{a}_4{\color{blue}+a_1(\overline{a}_1+\overline{a}_3)\overline{b}_2b_4}\quad \text{and} \quad   Q=a_1\overline{a}_2+(a_1+a_3)\overline{a}_4{\color{blue}+a_1\overline{b}_2b_4}.
    \]
    Note that $\mathrm{val}(\overline{b}_2)\geq \dfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(\overline{a}_2)$ and $\mathrm{val}(b_4)\geq \dfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(\overline{a}_4)$. Thus, we have
    \begin{align*}    \mathrm{val}(a_1\overline{a}_1\overline{b}_2b_4)\geq &\mathrm{val}(N(a_1))+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(\overline{a}_2)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(\overline{a}_4)\geq
    \min\{\mathrm{val}(N(a_1)\overline{a}_2), \mathrm{val}(N(a_1)\overline{a}_4)\},\\                            
    \mathrm{val}(a_1\overline{a}_3\overline{b}_2b_4)\geq &\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(N(a_1)\overline{a}_2)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(N(a_3)\overline{a}_4)   
    \geq  \min\{\mathrm{val}(N(a_1)\overline{a}_2), \mathrm{val}(N(a_3)\overline{a}_4)\},\\
                                 
                 
        
     
    \mathrm{val}(a_1\overline{b}_2b_4)\geq&\mathrm{val}(a_1)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(\overline{a}_2)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{val}(\overline{a}_4) \geq  \min\{\mathrm{val}(a_1\overline{a}_2),\mathrm{val}(a_1\overline{a}_4)\}.                          
    \end{align*}
    In other words, the part highlighted in blue in each of $P$ and $Q$ does not affect the minimum of the valuations of the terms in each of them. 

    When at least one of $n_2$ and $n_4$ is odd, the above inequalities are strict, and we can simply ignore the blue terms. We then observe that among the remaining terms, their Newton polytopes are triangles (2-simplices), and there is exactly one term for each extremal degree vector; therefore, by Lemma \ref{lem: non-zero coefficient}, we know that the minima of the valuations of the terms in $P$ and $Q$, respectively, are achieved generically.

    When both $n_2$ and $n_4$ are even, we know that $\mathrm{val}(a_2)=2\mathrm{val}(b_2)$ and $\mathrm{val}(a_4)=2\mathrm{val}(b_4)$. Thus, we may replace $\overline{a}_2$ by $b_2\overline{b}_2/2$ and replace $\overline{a}_4$ with $b_4\overline{b}_4/2$ in $P$ and $Q$ when computing their valuations. Now with respect to the variables $a_1$, $b_2$, $a_3$, and $b_4$, the Newton polytopes of these modified $P$ and $Q$ are still triangles, and there is still exactly one term for each extremal degree vector. In particular, these terms are in the black part of the expansion. Thus, by Lemma \ref{lem: non-zero coefficient}, we know that the minima of the valuations of the terms in $P$ and $Q$, respectively, are achieved generically. In conclusion, there is a non-empty open subset of $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ over which
    
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    
     
                                                                                                                                                                                                                          
       
                                                                                                                                 
       
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             
    \begin{align*}
    \mathrm{val}(P)=&\min\{2n_1+n_2,2n_1+n_4, 2n_3+n_4\}=p,
    \\
                                           
    \mathrm{val}(Q)=&\min\{n_1+n_2,n_1+n_4,n_3+n_4\}=q.
    \end{align*}
    Plugging them back into the valuations of $a'_i$'s yields the desired formulas.


    When $m=4$, we get that
    \[
    a'_1=\frac{a_2N(a_3)a_4}{\overline{P}}, \quad b'_1=\frac{(a_1\overline{a}_4b_2-a_1a_2b_4+a_3\overline{a}_4b_2)\overline{a}_3}{P}, \quad a'_2=\frac{\overline{P}}{\overline{Q}}
    \]
    \[
    a'_3=\frac{N(Q)}{P}, \quad b'_3=\frac{(\overline{a}_1b_2+\overline{a}_1b_4+\overline{a}_3b_4)Q}{P}, \quad a'_4=\frac{\overline{a}_1a_2a_3}{\overline{Q}}
    \]
    where
    \[
    P=N(a_1)\overline{a}_2+N(a_1+a_3)\overline{a}_4{\color{blue}+a_1(\overline{a}_1+\overline{a}_3)\overline{b}_2b_4t^2} \quad \text{and} \quad Q=a_1\overline{a}_2+(a_1+a_3)\overline{a}_4{\color{blue}+a_1\overline{b}_2b_4t^2}.
    \]
    An argument similar to the $m=2$ case shows that there is a non-empty open subset of $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ such that $\mathrm{val}(P)=2p$ and $\mathrm{val}(Q)=2q$. Note that they carry an extra factor $2$ because $\mathrm{val}(a_i)=2n_i$ for each $i$; but then we also need to divide $\mathrm{val}(a'_i)$ by $2$ to get to $n'_i$. Thus, we conclude the desired formulas again.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proposition-16: body_md -->
\label{prop:B2'} If $y_\jmath(a'_1)y_\imath(a'_2)y_\jmath(a'_3)y_\imath(a'_4)=y_\imath(a_1)y_\jmath(a_2)y_\imath(a_3)y_\jmath(a_4)$, then there is a non-empty open subset in $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$, over which
    \[
    n'_1=n_2+n_3+n_4-p, \quad n'_2=2p-q, \quad n'_3=q-p, \quad n'_4=n_1+2n_2+n_3-q,
    \]
    where 
    \[
    p:=\min\{n_1+n_2,n_1+n_4,n_3+n_4\} \quad \text{and} \quad q:=\min\{n_1+2n_2,n_1+2n_4,n_3+2n_4\}.
    \]
<!-- end proposition-16 -->
<!-- proposition-17: body_md -->
\label{prop: G2} If $y_\imath(a'_1)y_\jmath(a'_2)y_\imath(a'_3)y_\jmath(a'_4)y_\imath(a'_5)y_\jmath(a'_6)=y_\jmath(a_1)y_\imath(a_2)y_\jmath(a_3)y_\imath(a_4)y_\jmath(a_5)y_\imath(a_6)$,
    then there is a non-empty open subset of $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$, over which
    \[
    n'_1=n_2+3n_3+2n_4+3n_5+n_6-p, \quad n'_2=p-q, \quad n'_3=3q-p-r,
    \]
    \[
    n'_4=r-q-s, \quad n'_5=3s-r, \quad n'_6=n_1+n_2+2n_3+n_4+n_5-s,
    \]
    where
    \begin{align*}
    p:=&\min\left\{\begin{array}{c}3n_1+2n_2+3n_5+n_6,\quad 3n_1+2n_4+3n_5+n_6, \quad 3n_3+2n_4+3n_5+n_6,\\
    3n_1+2n_2+3n_3+n_4, \quad 3n_1+2n_2+3n_3+n_6
    \end{array}\right\},\\
    q:=&\min\left\{\begin{array}{c}2n_1+2n_2+3n_3+n_4, \quad 2n_1+2n_2+3n_3+
    n_6, \quad 2n_1+2n_2+3n_5+n_6, \\
    2n_1+2n_4+3n_5+n_6, \quad 2n_3+2n_4+3n_5+n_6\end{array}\right\},\\
    r:=&\min\left\{\begin{array}{c}3n_1+3n_2+6n_3+2n_4, \quad 3n_1+3n_2+6n_3+2n_6, \quad 3n_1+3n_2+6n_5+2n_6,\\
    3n_1+3n_4+6n_5+2n_6, \quad 3n_3+3n_4+6n_5+2n_6\end{array}\right\},\\
    s:=&\min\left\{\begin{array}{c}n_1+n_2+2n_3+n_4, \quad n_1+n_2+2n_3+n_6, \quad n_1+n_2+2n_5+n_6,\\
    n_1+n_4+2n_5+n_6,\quad n_3+n_4+2n_5+n_6
    \end{array}\right\}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-17 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
There is only one case to check: $D_4^{(3)}$. In this case, the field automorphism $\gamma$ on $\mathcal{K}$ is of order $3$. Let $\imath=\{1,3,4\}$ be the $\tau$-orbit of size $3$ and let $\jmath=\{2\}$ be the $\tau$-fixed vertex. Let us adopt the notations $\dot{x}$, $\ddot{x}$, $N(x)$, and $C(x)$ in Example \ref{exmp: polynomial}. Then                                                                                                                                                                                                                     
    \begin{align*}
        &y_\jmath(a_1)y_\imath(a_2)y_\jmath(a_3)y_\imath(a_4)y_\jmath(a_5)y_\imath(a_6)\\
        =&y_2(a_1)y_1(a_2)y_3(\dot{a}_2)y_4(\ddot{a}_2)y_2(a_3)y_1(a_4)y_3(\dot{a}_4)y_4(\ddot{a}_4)y_2(a_5)y_1(a_6)y_3(\dot{a}_6)y_4(\ddot{a}_6),
    \end{align*}
    with $a_1,a_3,a_5\in \mathcal{K}^\gamma=k(\!(t^3)\!)$ and $a_2,a_4,a_6\in \mathcal{K}$. In order to get to $y_\imath(a'_1)y_\jmath(a'_2)y_\imath(a'_3)y_\jmath(a'_4)y_\imath(a'_5)y_\jmath(a'_6)$, we can apply the following braid moves in $W$:
    \begin{align*}
        &{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}\\
        =&{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}\\
        =&{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}\\
        =&{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}\\
        =&{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}\\
        =&{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}={\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}{\color{blue}s_{1}}{\color{blue}s_{3}}{\color{blue}s_{4}}{\color{red}s_{2}}.
    \end{align*}
    By applying Eq. \eqref{eq: Lusztig transform} repetitively, we get
    \[
    a'_1=\frac{a_2a_3\dot{a}_4\ddot{a}_4a_5a_6}{P}, \quad a'_2=\frac{N(P)}{Q},\quad a'_3=\frac{Q}{PR}, \quad a'_4=\frac{N(R)}{QS} \quad a'_5=\frac{S}{R}, \quad a'_6=\frac{a_1a_3^2a_5N(a_2a_4)}{S}
    \]
    where
    \begin{align*}
    P=&a_3\dot{a}_4\ddot{a}_4a_5a_6+a_1(\dot{a}_2+\dot{a}_4)(\ddot{a}_2+\ddot{a}_4)a_5a_6+a_1\dot{a}_2\ddot{a}_2a_3(a_4+a_6),\\
    Q=&a_1^2N\left(\dot{a}_2\ddot{a}_2a_3(a_4+a_6)+(\dot{a}_2+\dot{a}_4)(\ddot{a}_2+\ddot{a}_4)a_5a_6\right)+a_3^2a_5^3N(a_4^2a_6){\color{blue}+2a_1a_3a_5^3N(a_2+a_4)N(a_4a_6)}\\
    &{\color{blue}+a_1a_3^2a_5^2N(a_4)\left(C(a_2\dot{a}_2\ddot{a}_4^2a_6\dot{a}_6)+N(a_2)C(a_4\dot{a}_6\ddot{a}_6)+C(a_2\dot{a}_2\ddot{a}_4)N(a_6)+2N(a_2a_6)\right)},\\
    R=&a_1a_3^2N(a_2)(\dot{a}_4+\dot{a}_6)(\ddot{a}_4+\ddot{a}_6)+a_1a_5^2\dot{a}_6\ddot{a}_6N(a_2+a_4)+a_3a_5^2\dot{a}_6\ddot{a}_6N(a_4)\\
    &{\color{blue}+a_1a_3a_5\left(N(a_2)(\dot{a}_4\ddot{a}_6+\dot{a}_6\ddot{a}_4+2\dot{a}_6\ddot{a}_6)+\dot{a}_6\ddot{a}_6C(a_2\ddot{a}_2\dot{a}_4)+a_2(\ddot{a}_2\dot{a}_4^2\ddot{a}_6+\dot{a}_2\ddot{a}_4^2\dot{a}_6)\right)},\\
    S=&a_1a_3^2N(a_2)N(a_4+a_6)+a_1a_5^2N(a_2+a_4)N(a_6)+a_3a_5^2N(a_4a_6)\\
    &{\color{blue}+a_1a_3a_5\left(C(a_2\dot{a}_2\ddot{a}_4^2a_6\dot{a}_6)+N(a_2)C(a_4\dot{a}_6\ddot{a}_6)+C(a_2\dot{a}_2\ddot{a}_4)N(a_6)+2N(a_2a_6)\right)}.
    \end{align*}

                                                                                                                                                                                               
    By using a computer program, we see that the Newton polytope of each of $P,Q,R,S$ is a $4$-dimensional simplex; we highlight the terms whose degree vectors are not extremal in blue above. It is not hard to see that for each of $P,Q,R,S$ and for each extremal degree vector $d$ within, there is exactly one term, and it has coefficient $1$. Thus, by Lemma \ref{lem: non-zero coefficient}, we can conclude that                                                                                                                                                                               
    \[
    \mathrm{val}(P)=p, \quad \mathrm{val}(Q)=3q, \quad \mathrm{val}(R)=r, \quad \mathrm{val}(S)=3s.
    \]
    (Note that $\mathrm{val}(a_i)=3n_i$ when $i$ is odd and $\mathrm{val}(a_i)=n_i$ when $i$ is even.) Plugging them into the rational expressions of the $a'_i$'s yields the desired formulas.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proposition-18: body_md -->
\label{prop:G2'} If $y_\jmath(a'_1)y_\imath(a'_2)y_\jmath(a'_3)y_\imath(a'_4)y_\jmath(a'_5)y_\imath(a'_6)=y_\imath(a_1)y_\jmath(a_2)y_\imath(a_3)y_\jmath(a_4)y_\imath(a_5)y_\jmath(a_6)$,
    then there is a non-empty open subset of $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$, over which
    \[
    n'_1=n_2+n_3+2n_4+n_5+n_6-p, \quad n'_2=3p-q, \quad n'_3=q-p-r,
    \]
    \[
    n'_4=3r-q-s, \quad n'_5=s-r, \quad n'_6=n_1+3n_2+2n_3+3n_4+n_5-s,
    \]
    where
    \begin{align*}
    p:=&\min\left\{\begin{array}{c}n_3+2n_4+n_5+n_6, \quad n_1+2n_4+n_5+n_6, \quad n_1+2n_2+n_5+n_6,\\
    n_1+2n_2+n_3+n_4,\quad n_1+2n_2+n_3+n_6
    \end{array}\right\},\\
    q:=&\min\left\{\begin{array}{c}2n_3+6n_4+3n_5+3n_6, \quad 2n_1+6n_2+3n_5+3n_6, \quad 2n_1+6n_4+3n_5+3n_6,\\
    2n_1+6n_2+3n_3+3n_4, \quad 2n_1+6n_2+3n_3+3n_6\end{array}\right\},\\
    r:=&\min\left\{\begin{array}{c}n_1+3n_2+2n_3+2n_4, \quad n_1+3n_2+2n_3+2n_6, \quad n_1+3n_2+2n_5+2n_6, \\
    n_1+3n_4+2n_5+2n_6, \quad n_3+2n_5+3n_4+2n_6\end{array}\right\},\\
    s:=&\min\left\{\begin{array}{c}n_1+3n_2+2n_3+3n_4, \quad n_1+3n_2+2n_3+3n_6, \quad n_1+3n_2+2n_5+3n_6,\\
    n_1+3n_4+2n_5+3n_6, \quad n_3+3n_4+2n_5+3n_6
    \end{array}\right\}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-18 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-MV}
    Suppose $\imath_\bullet$ and $\imath'_\bullet$ differ by a single braid move, and $n_\bullet$ and $n'_\bullet$ satisfy the respective Lusztig transformation, i.e. the formulas listed in Propositions \ref{prop: A1xA1}, \ref{prop: A2}, \ref{prop: B2}, \ref{prop:B2'}, \ref{prop: G2}, and \ref{prop:G2'}, then $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ and $A^{\imath'_\bullet}(n'_\bullet)$ have a non-empty intersection that is dense in each of them.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
From the transformation formulas we see that as long as the expressions in the denominators of the arguments inside $y_{\imath'}$'s are non-zero, the product $y=y_{\imath_1}y_{\imath_2}\cdots y_{\imath_N}$ can be factored as $y_{\imath'_1}y_{\imath'_2}\cdots y_{\imath'_N}$ as well. Thus, there is a non-empty open subset of $\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ whose image $[g]=[\eta_{w_0}^{-1}(y)]$ lies inside $A^{\imath'_\bullet}(n'_\bullet)$. Since the map $\Phi:\mathcal{A}^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)\rightarrow A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ is surjective (Theorem \ref{prop: surjective}), it follows that the intersection $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)\cap A^{\imath'_\bullet}(n'_\bullet)$ is dense inside $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$. The same argument shows that the intersection is dense inside $A^{\imath'_\bullet}(n'_\bullet)$.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{cor_MV_Lusztig_transf}
    \begin{enumerate}
\item Fix a reduced expression $\imath_\bullet$ of $w_0\in W^\tau$,  there is a one-to-one correspondence between sequence $n_\bullet$ of non-negative integers such that $\sum_s n_s \beta_s^{\imath_\bullet}=\mu$,  and irreducible components of $\overline{S_\mu\cap T_0}$, sending $n_\bullet$ to $\overline{A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)}$
 \item  Given any two Lusztig data $(\imath_\bullet, n_\bullet)$ and $(\imath'_\bullet, n'_\bullet)$, 
        if $n'_\bullet=R_{\imath_\bullet}^{ \imath'_\bullet}(n_\bullet) $, 
   then
        $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)\cap A^{\imath'_\bullet}(n'_\bullet)$ contains a dense subset in each of $A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)$ and $A^{\imath'_\bullet}(n'_\bullet)$, and 
        $\overline{A^{\imath_\bullet}(n_\bullet)}=\overline{A^{\imath'_\bullet}(n'_\bullet)}$. 
     
   \end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
Part 1) follows from Theorem \ref{thm_MV_constr}, and part 2) follows from Theorem \ref{thm:main-MV}.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
